<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 08 (membrane)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cell surface receptors (e.g., CD4, CD8) in T cell development, selection, and function, particularly in the context of positive and negative selection in the thymus, and their subsequent roles in recognizing MHC molecules and interacting with other immune cells. (理解T细胞发育过程中，特定细胞表面受体（如CD4、CD8）的作用，特别是在胸腺中的阳性选择和阴性选择，以及它们随后在识别MHC分子和与其他免疫细胞相互作用中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, severe headache, and photophobia. He is a known HIV-positive patient with a CD4+ T cell count of 150 cells/µL, currently not on antiretroviral therapy. Physical examination reveals nuchal rigidity and altered mental status. Lumbar puncture reveals cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with elevated protein, low glucose, and numerous neutrophils. Gram stain of the CSF shows Gram-negative diplococci. Empiric treatment with ceftriaxone and vancomycin is initiated. Despite treatment, the patient's condition deteriorates rapidly, leading to septic shock and multi-organ failure. Postmortem examination of the brain reveals extensive purulent meningitis. Microscopic analysis of the brain tissue shows numerous bacteria, and immunohistochemical staining reveals a marked absence of CD4+ T cells infiltrating the meninges and brain parenchyma, while CD8+ T cells are present but appear functionally impaired. Further analysis of the patient's peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significantly reduced proportion of CD4+ T cells expressing the T cell receptor (TCR) complex, specifically lacking the CD3 component, which is essential for signal transduction following TCR engagement.

Which of the following cellular components is most likely deficient in this patient, contributing to his severe susceptibility to this infection?

(A) CD8+ T cells capable of recognizing MHC class I molecules presenting bacterial antigens.
(B) B cells capable of producing antibodies against the bacterial capsule.
(C) CD4+ T cells capable of recognizing MHC class II molecules presenting bacterial antigens.
(D) Natural killer (NK) cells capable of mediating antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).
(E) Dendritic cells capable of presenting bacterial antigens to T cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male, HIV-positive, CD4 count 150 cells/µL (severe immunodeficiency), not on ART. This immediately points towards a T cell-mediated immunodeficiency.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Symptoms (fever, chills, headache, photophobia, nuchal rigidity, altered mental status) and CSF findings (high protein, low glucose, neutrophils, Gram-negative diplococci) are classic for bacterial meningitis, likely caused by *Neisseria meningitidis*.
*   **Treatment Failure & Outcome:** Rapid deterioration despite appropriate antibiotics suggests a profound immune defect preventing effective clearance of the infection.
*   **Pathology:** Postmortem findings show extensive meningitis. Immunohistochemistry reveals a marked absence of CD4+ T cells in the CNS infiltrate, while CD8+ T cells are present but impaired. This highlights the critical role of CD4+ T cells in controlling this specific infection.
*   **Lymphocyte Analysis:** Peripheral blood analysis shows reduced CD4+ T cells expressing the TCR complex, specifically lacking the CD3 component. This points to a defect in T cell development or signaling, potentially related to the severe HIV infection affecting T cell maturation or function.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the deficient cellular component contributing to susceptibility.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **HIV and CD4+ T Cells:** HIV primarily infects and destroys CD4+ T helper cells, leading to profound immunodeficiency (AIDS). A CD4 count below 200 cells/µL indicates severe immunosuppression.
*   **T Cell Development & Selection:** T cells develop in the thymus. Immature T cells (thymocytes) express TCRs. Positive selection occurs when thymocytes whose TCRs weakly recognize self-MHC molecules (either class I or class II) receive survival signals. Thymocytes expressing TCRs that recognize MHC class II molecules become CD4+ T cells (helper T cells), while those recognizing MHC class I molecules become CD8+ T cells (cytotoxic T cells). Negative selection eliminates thymocytes with high affinity for self-antigens presented by MHC molecules, preventing autoimmunity.
*   **T Cell Function:** CD4+ T helper cells are crucial for orchestrating adaptive immune responses. They recognize antigens presented by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells and macrophages. Upon activation, they differentiate into various subsets (Th1, Th2, Th17, etc.) and secrete cytokines that activate other immune cells, including B cells (for antibody production) and CD8+ T cells (for cytotoxic activity) and macrophages.
*   **Role in Bacterial Meningitis:** CD4+ T cells play a critical role in controlling bacterial meningitis. They help activate macrophages and B cells, leading to opsonization, phagocytosis, and antibody production against the bacteria. Their absence severely impairs the host's ability to clear the infection.
*   **CD3 Complex:** The CD3 complex is a multi-subunit protein complex associated with the TCR on the surface of T cells. It is essential for signal transduction following TCR engagement with peptide-MHC complexes. Without a functional CD3 complex, the TCR cannot effectively initiate intracellular signaling cascades required for T cell activation. The vignette mentions a lack of the CD3 component, suggesting a defect in T cell signaling, which can occur in severe HIV infection or other T cell disorders, further impairing CD4+ T cell function.
*   **Specific Pathogen:** *Neisseria meningitidis* is a common cause of bacterial meningitis. While antibodies are important, cell-mediated immunity involving CD4+ T cells is crucial for controlling intracellular bacteria and coordinating the overall immune response, especially in the CNS.

##### 3. Synthesis:
The patient's severe HIV infection (CD4 count 150) has led to a profound deficiency in CD4+ T cells. These cells are essential for recognizing bacterial antigens presented on MHC class II molecules by APCs, activating macrophages, and helping B cells produce antibodies. The absence of CD4+ T cells in the CNS infiltrate and the impaired function of remaining CD8+ T cells (which often require CD4 help) explain the patient's inability to control the *N. meningitidis* infection despite antibiotic treatment. The finding of reduced CD3 expression further indicates a functional defect in the remaining CD4+ T cells, consistent with the effects of HIV on T cell signaling. Therefore, the deficiency in CD4+ T cells capable of recognizing MHC class II molecules presenting bacterial antigens is the most critical factor contributing to the patient's susceptibility and poor outcome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **CD8+ T cells capable of recognizing MHC class I molecules presenting bacterial antigens.** While CD8+ T cells are important for killing infected cells (especially virally infected cells), their primary role in extracellular bacterial infections like meningitis is less central than CD4+ T cells. Furthermore, the vignette specifically notes the *absence* of CD4+ T cells and *impaired* function of CD8+ T cells, suggesting CD4+ T cell deficiency is the primary problem. CD8+ T cells recognize antigens presented on MHC class I, typically found on nucleated cells, not primarily on extracellular bacteria or APCs presenting extracellular antigens.
- (B) **B cells capable of producing antibodies against the bacterial capsule.** B cells are crucial for producing antibodies, which are important for opsonization and complement activation against encapsulated bacteria like *N. meningitidis*. However, B cell activation and antibody production often require help from CD4+ T helper cells (specifically T follicular helper cells). The severe CD4+ T cell deficiency would impair this T cell help, leading to suboptimal antibody responses, but the *primary* defect highlighted by the clinical picture and pathology is the lack of CD4+ T cells themselves.
- (D) **Natural killer (NK) cells capable of mediating antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).** NK cells are part of the innate immune system and primarily target virus-infected cells or tumor cells lacking MHC class I expression. While they can mediate ADCC if antibodies are present, they are not the primary effector cells for controlling bacterial meningitis, especially extracellular bacteria. Their role is less significant compared to T cells and phagocytes in this context.
- (E) **Dendritic cells capable of presenting bacterial antigens to T cells.** Dendritic cells are professional APCs crucial for initiating adaptive immune responses. They capture antigens, process them, and present them on MHC molecules to T cells. While dendritic cell function might be indirectly affected by the overall immune dysregulation in severe HIV, the primary defect described in the vignette is the lack of functional CD4+ T cells to *respond* to the antigens presented by dendritic cells (and other APCs). The vignette focuses on the effector T cell deficiency, not the APCs themselves.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - CD4+ T cells | Distractor (A) - CD8+ T cells | Distractor (B) - B cells | Distractor (D) - NK cells | Distractor (E) - Dendritic Cells |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :---------------------- | :------------------------ | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Role**         | Helper function, orchestrate adaptive immunity | Cytotoxic function, kill infected cells | Antibody production, humoral immunity | Innate immunity, kill target cells | Antigen presentation, initiate adaptive immunity |
| **MHC Restriction**      | MHC Class II                      | MHC Class I                  | N/A (present antigen)    | N/A                       | MHC Class I & II                  |
| **Key Cytokines**        | IL-2, IFN-γ, IL-4, IL-17, etc.    | IFN-γ, TNF-α, Granzyme B     | Antibodies              | IFN-γ, TNF-α              | N/A                               |
| **Role in Meningitis**  | Critical for macrophage activation, B cell help | Less direct role          | Important for opsonization | Limited role              | Essential for antigen presentation |
| **Deficiency Impact**    | Impaired cell-mediated immunity, B cell help | Impaired killing of infected cells | Impaired antibody response | Impaired killing of certain targets | Impaired initiation of adaptive immunity |
| **Vignette Evidence**    | Absent in CNS, reduced CD3 expression, low CD4 count | Present but impaired      | Function impaired due to lack of CD4 help | Not primary defect        | Not primary defect                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Severe HIV infection leads to profound depletion of CD4+ T helper cells, which are critical for orchestrating immune responses against many pathogens, including bacteria causing meningitis. CD4+ T cells recognize antigens presented on MHC class II molecules. Their absence impairs macrophage activation, B cell help, and overall control of infection. Differentiating the roles of CD4+ vs. CD8+ T cells, B cells, NK cells, and APCs in specific immune responses is crucial for understanding immunodeficiencies and infectious disease pathogenesis.

<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 09 (anchorage.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of stromal cells (specifically, the thymic epithelial cell-derived MHC class II molecule) in positive selection of CD4+ T cells during thymopoiesis, and the consequences of its deficiency. (理解基质细胞（特别是胸腺上皮细胞衍生的MHC II分子）在胸腺细胞发育过程中CD4+ T细胞阳性选择中的作用，以及其缺乏的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant presents with recurrent severe bacterial infections (Streptococcus pneumoniae, Staphylococcus aureus) and opportunistic fungal infections (Candida albicans) starting shortly after birth. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died in infancy from severe infections. The infant has failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea. Immunological workup reveals markedly reduced numbers of peripheral CD4+ T cells (<100 cells/µL) but normal numbers of CD8+ T cells, B cells, and NK cells. T-cell receptor (TCR) beta chain gene rearrangement analysis shows a significant reduction in Vβ diversity, particularly involving Vβ segments that pair with Jβ segments encoding for TCRs recognizing MHC class II molecules. Flow cytometry analysis of thymocytes reveals a severe block in T-cell development at the double-positive (CD4+CD8+) stage, with a marked reduction in cells expressing both CD4 and CD8. Furthermore, analysis of the thymic microenvironment shows a deficiency in MHC class II expression on thymic epithelial cells.

(A) Deficiency in RAG1/RAG2 leading to impaired V(D)J recombination.
(B) Deficiency in CD3 signaling complex leading to impaired T-cell maturation.
(C) Deficiency in MHC class II expression on thymic epithelial cells leading to impaired positive selection of CD4+ T cells.
(D) Deficiency in the ZAP-70 kinase leading to impaired signal transduction after TCR engagement.
(E) Deficiency in the common gamma chain (γc) shared by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors leading to impaired T-cell proliferation and survival.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial and opportunistic fungal infections in infancy**: Suggests a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a severe T-cell defect.
- **Reduced CD4+ T cells, normal CD8+, B cells, NK cells**: Points specifically to a defect affecting CD4+ T-cell development or survival, rather than a global lymphocyte deficiency like SCID affecting all lineages.
- **Reduced Vβ diversity, especially for MHC class II-restricted TCRs**: Indicates a problem with the selection process for TCRs that recognize MHC class II.
- **Developmental block at the CD4+CD8+ (double-positive) stage**: This is the stage where positive and negative selection occur in the thymus.
- **Deficiency in MHC class II expression on thymic epithelial cells**: This is the crucial clue. Thymic epithelial cells (TECs) express MHC class I and MHC class II molecules. MHC class II molecules on TECs are essential for positive selection of CD4+ T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Development**: T-cell precursors migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus. In the thymus, they undergo maturation, including V(D)J recombination of TCR genes (alpha and beta chains) and selection processes.
- **Positive Selection**: Occurs in the thymic cortex. Double-positive (DP) thymocytes (CD4+CD8+) express pre-TCRs and then TCRs. If the TCR can bind weakly to self-MHC molecules presented by thymic epithelial cells (TECs) or dendritic cells, the thymocyte receives a survival signal. MHC class II molecules on TECs are critical for positive selection of CD4+ T cells, ensuring that mature CD4+ T cells can recognize antigens presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) expressing MHC class II. MHC class I molecules on TECs are critical for positive selection of CD8+ T cells.
- **Negative Selection**: Occurs primarily in the thymic medulla. Thymocytes that bind too strongly to self-MHC/peptide complexes presented by TECs and dendritic cells receive apoptotic signals, eliminating potentially autoreactive T cells.
- **MHC Class II Deficiency**: If MHC class II molecules are deficient on TECs (e.g., due to mutations in MHC class II genes or associated invariant chain genes like CD74), DP thymocytes expressing TCRs specific for MHC class II cannot receive the necessary survival signals during positive selection. Consequently, these cells undergo apoptosis, leading to a severe reduction or absence of mature CD4+ T cells in the periphery. CD8+ T-cell development, which relies on MHC class I for positive selection, is typically less affected or unaffected. This condition is known as MHC class II deficiency or Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome.
- **Clinical Manifestations**: The lack of functional CD4+ T cells leads to impaired cell-mediated immunity and defective T-helper function for B cells. Patients suffer from recurrent severe infections, particularly with intracellular pathogens (bacteria, fungi, viruses) that require T-cell help for clearance.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Deficiency in RAG1/RAG2**: RAG1 and RAG2 are essential enzymes for V(D)J recombination. Deficiency leads to a block in lymphocyte development *before* TCR gene rearrangement is complete, resulting in a lack of both T cells and B cells (SCID). This patient has normal B cells and CD8+ T cells, ruling out RAG deficiency.
- (B) **Deficiency in CD3 signaling complex**: The CD3 complex is part of the TCR complex and is essential for signal transduction upon TCR engagement. Deficiency leads to impaired T-cell maturation and function, often presenting as SCID or severe T-cell deficiency. However, it typically affects both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells globally, and the specific block at the DP stage with MHC class II deficiency is not characteristic.
- (D) **Deficiency in ZAP-70 kinase**: ZAP-70 is a tyrosine kinase crucial for TCR signaling. Deficiency causes an autosomal recessive form of SCID, characterized by a lack of mature T cells (both CD4+ and CD8+) and NK cells, but normal B cells. This patient has normal CD8+ T cells and NK cells, making ZAP-70 deficiency unlikely.
- (E) **Deficiency in the common gamma chain (γc)**: The γc is a shared signaling subunit for receptors of several cytokines crucial for lymphocyte development and function (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21). Deficiency causes X-linked SCID (X-SCID), characterized by a lack of T cells and NK cells, and often reduced B cells. This patient has normal CD8+ T cells and NK cells, ruling out γc deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | MHC Class II Deficiency (Correct Answer) | RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency (A) | CD3 Deficiency (B) | ZAP-70 Deficiency (D) | γc Deficiency (E) |
|-----------------------------|------------------------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------|-----------------------|-------------------|
| **Affected Lymphocytes**    | Primarily CD4+ T cells                  | T cells, B cells         | T cells            | T cells, NK cells     | T cells, NK cells |
| **B Cells**                 | Normal                                   | Absent/Reduced           | Normal             | Normal                | Normal/Reduced    |
| **NK Cells**                | Normal                                   | Normal                  | Normal             | Absent/Reduced        | Absent/Reduced    |
| **Developmental Block**     | DP stage (CD4+CD8+)                     | Pre-TCR/TCR stage        | Variable           | Pre-TCR/TCR stage     | Pre-TCR/TCR stage |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Deficient on TECs                       | Normal                  | Normal             | Normal                | Normal            |
| **Vβ Diversity**            | Reduced (MHC II-restricted)             | Absent                  | Variable           | Absent                | Absent            |
| **Inheritance**             | Autosomal recessive                     | Autosomal recessive      | Autosomal recessive| Autosomal recessive   | X-linked          |
| **Clinical Presentation**   | Severe bacterial/fungal infections       | Severe infections (SCID) | Severe infections  | Severe infections (SCID)| Severe infections (SCID)|

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key role of MHC class II molecules on thymic epithelial cells is to positively select CD4+ T cells. Deficiency in MHC class II expression leads to a selective absence of CD4+ T cells, resulting in susceptibility to infections requiring T-helper cell function. Differentiate this from SCID syndromes (RAG, CD3, ZAP-70, γc deficiencies) which affect multiple lymphocyte lineages or both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells.

<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 10 (terminal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of negative selection of T cells in the thymus, specifically the role of Autoimmune Regulator (AIRE) gene and its impact on central tolerance, leading to autoimmune diseases like Autoimmune Polyendocrinopathy-Candidiasis-Ectodermal Dystrophy (APECED) when mutated. (理解胸腺中T细胞的阴性选择过程，特别是自身免疫调节基因(AIRE)的作用及其对中心耐受的影响，以及AIRE基因突变导致自身免疫性疾病，如自身免疫多内分泌综合征-念珠菌病-异位性皮层发育不良(APECED)的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman presents with a 5-year history of recurrent oral thrush (candidiasis), hypothyroidism, and type 1 diabetes mellitus. She also reports brittle nails and sparse hair. Her medical history is significant for early-onset hypoparathyroidism diagnosed at age 10. Family history reveals that her younger brother died at age 15 due to adrenal insufficiency. Laboratory tests show elevated anti-thyroid peroxidase antibodies, anti-GAD65 antibodies, and anti-21-hydroxylase antibodies. A complete blood count reveals lymphopenia. Flow cytometry analysis of her peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a markedly reduced number of CD4+CD8+ double-positive (DP) T cells in the thymus, and an increased proportion of CD4+ single-positive (SP) T cells expressing markers of recent thymic emigrants (CD62LhiCD44lo). Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *AIRE* gene.

Which of the following cellular processes is most directly impaired in this patient, leading to her clinical presentation?
(A) Positive selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymic cortex.
(B) Negative selection of self-reactive T cells in the thymic medulla.
(C) V(D)J recombination of T cell receptor genes in the bone marrow.
(D) Migration of mature CD4+ T cells from the thymus to the peripheral blood.
(E) Differentiation of regulatory T cells (Tregs) in the peripheral lymphoid organs.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old woman with recurrent candidiasis, hypothyroidism, type 1 diabetes, brittle nails/hair, early-onset hypoparathyroidism, and family history of adrenal insufficiency. This constellation of autoimmune endocrine disorders is highly suggestive of APECED.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated autoantibodies (anti-TPO, anti-GAD65, anti-21-hydroxylase) confirm autoimmune attacks on multiple endocrine glands. Lymphopenia is common in APECED.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Reduced CD4+CD8+ DP T cells and increased recent thymic emigrants (CD4+ SP, CD62LhiCD44lo) suggest a problem within the thymus affecting T cell development and selection.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous *AIRE* gene mutation is the definitive diagnosis for APECED.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cellular process most directly impaired by the *AIRE* mutation, linking the genetic defect to the pathophysiology of the disease.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **AIRE Function:** The Autoimmune Regulator (AIRE) protein is a transcription factor expressed primarily in medullary thymic epithelial cells (mTECs). Its crucial role is to induce the expression of tissue-restricted antigens (TRAs) – proteins normally found only in specific peripheral tissues (e.g., insulin from pancreatic beta cells, thyroglobulin from the thyroid, adrenal steroidogenic enzymes) – within the thymus.
- **Negative Selection:** During T cell development, thymocytes undergo positive selection in the cortex (ensuring TCR can bind MHC) and negative selection in the medulla. Negative selection eliminates T cells that strongly recognize self-antigens presented by mTECs. This process is critical for establishing central tolerance and preventing autoimmunity.
- **AIRE and Negative Selection:** By expressing TRAs, AIRE allows mTECs to present these self-antigens on MHC class I and II molecules to developing T cells (both CD4+ and CD8+). Thymocytes that react strongly to these self-antigens are deleted (apoptosis) or diverted into the regulatory T cell lineage.
- **AIRE Deficiency (APECED):** Mutations in the *AIRE* gene lead to deficient expression of TRAs in the thymus. Consequently, many self-reactive T cells that would normally be eliminated during negative selection escape the thymus and enter the periphery. These autoreactive T cells then attack various endocrine tissues, leading to the characteristic autoimmune polyendocrinopathy seen in APECED. The clinical features (candidiasis, hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency, diabetes, thyroiditis) reflect the specific tissues where AIRE-regulated antigens are expressed. The flow cytometry findings (reduced DP cells, increased recent emigrants) are consistent with impaired negative selection, as more cells pass through the selection process without being deleted.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Positive selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymic cortex.** Positive selection occurs in the cortex and ensures that T cells can recognize self-MHC molecules. AIRE is primarily expressed in the medulla, not the cortex, and its function relates to negative selection, not positive selection. Impairment here would lead to a lack of functional T cells, not autoimmunity.
- (C) **V(D)J recombination of T cell receptor genes in the bone marrow.** V(D)J recombination is the process of generating TCR diversity in developing lymphocytes in the bone marrow (for B cells) or thymus (for T cells). AIRE acts *after* V(D)J recombination, during the selection process in the thymus. Defects in V(D)J recombination would affect TCR diversity, not specifically central tolerance mediated by AIRE.
- (D) **Migration of mature CD4+ T cells from the thymus to the peripheral blood.** While T cell development involves migration, AIRE's primary role is not in the final emigration step. The flow cytometry data (increased recent emigrants) suggests that cells *are* emigrating, but that the selection process *before* emigration is faulty.
- (E) **Differentiation of regulatory T cells (Tregs) in the peripheral lymphoid organs.** While some self-reactive T cells are diverted to the Treg lineage during negative selection in the thymus (a process also influenced by AIRE), the primary defect in APECED is the failure of *negative selection* (deletion of strongly self-reactive cells). Peripheral Treg differentiation is a separate, albeit related, mechanism for maintaining tolerance. The core problem in APECED is the escape of autoreactive cells due to faulty central tolerance.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature | APECED (AIRE Deficiency) | DiGeorge Syndrome (Thymic Hypoplasia) | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Primary Defect** | Impaired negative selection in thymus (AIRE mutation) | Thymic hypoplasia/aplasia | Defects in lymphocyte development (e.g., RAG1/2, JAK3, IL2RG) | Defect in phagocyte NADPH oxidase |
| **Affected Cell Type** | T cells (specifically autoreactive ones) | T cells (due to lack of thymus) | T cells, B cells, NK cells | Phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Autoimmune polyendocrinopathy, candidiasis | T cell deficiency, hypocalcemia (parathyroid aplasia), congenital heart defects | Severe recurrent infections (bacterial, viral, fungal) | Recurrent bacterial and fungal infections (catalase-positive organisms) |
| **Thymus Status** | Structurally normal, but function impaired | Hypoplastic or absent | Usually present, but lymphocyte development impaired | Normal |
| **AIRE Gene** | Mutated | Normal | Normal | Normal |
| **Tolerance Mechanism** | Central tolerance (negative selection) impaired | Central tolerance impaired (due to lack of T cell development) | Central tolerance impaired (due to lack of T cell development) | Innate immunity impaired |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
APECED is caused by mutations in the *AIRE* gene, leading to defective expression of tissue-restricted antigens in the thymic medulla. This impairs negative selection, allowing autoreactive T cells to escape the thymus and cause autoimmune disease. Key differentials include other autoimmune diseases (like Type 1 Diabetes, Hashimoto's thyroiditis) which can occur in APECED, and other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T cell development (like DiGeorge syndrome or SCID), but APECED is unique in its specific mechanism of impaired central tolerance due to AIRE deficiency and its characteristic constellation of autoimmune endocrine disorders.

<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 11 (molecule, B2M)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of β2-microglobulin (B2M) in MHC class I molecule assembly and its impact on CD8+ T cell function and susceptibility to certain infections. (理解β2-微球蛋白(B2M)在MHC I类分子组装中的作用及其对CD8+ T细胞功能和对某些感染的易感性的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male with a history of chronic kidney disease requiring dialysis presents with a persistent, non-productive cough and progressive dyspnea over the past month. He was recently diagnosed with a severe, disseminated infection caused by *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC). His CD4+ T cell count is 150 cells/µL, and his CD8+ T cell count is 500 cells/µL. Flow cytometry analysis reveals that his CD8+ T cells lack expression of MHC class I molecules on their surface. His serum β2-microglobulin level is markedly decreased. He has no history of HIV infection or other known immunodeficiency. He received the standard childhood vaccinations but has not received any adult vaccinations.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism explaining this patient's susceptibility to disseminated MAC infection?

(A) Defective production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by CD4+ T cells, impairing macrophage activation.
(B) Impaired antigen presentation by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(C) Defective assembly of MHC class I molecules due to insufficient β2-microglobulin, leading to impaired CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity and NK cell recognition.
(D) Defective production of interleukin-2 (IL-2) by CD4+ T cells, impairing T cell proliferation.
(E) Defective production of antibodies by B cells, leading to impaired opsonization and complement activation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male with chronic kidney disease (CKD) on dialysis. CKD is a known cause of secondary immunodeficiency.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Persistent cough, dyspnea, suggesting a pulmonary infection.
- **Infection:** Severe, disseminated *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC) infection. MAC is an opportunistic pathogen, often seen in immunocompromised individuals, particularly those with low CD4+ counts.
- **Immunological Status:** Low CD4+ count (150 cells/µL) and relatively normal CD8+ count (500 cells/µL). The CD4+ count is low enough to predispose to opportunistic infections like MAC.
- **Key Finding:** Flow cytometry shows CD8+ T cells lack MHC class I expression.
- **Biochemical Finding:** Markedly decreased serum β2-microglobulin (B2M).
- **Connecting the Clues:** CKD patients on dialysis often have low serum B2M levels due to its loss during dialysis. B2M is essential for the stable expression of MHC class I molecules on the cell surface. MHC class I molecules are crucial for presenting intracellular antigens (like viral or bacterial antigens) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). Lack of MHC class I on CD8+ T cells themselves is unusual but can occur in certain conditions. More importantly, the lack of MHC class I on *other* cells (like macrophages, epithelial cells) due to low B2M impairs the ability of CD8+ T cells to recognize and kill infected cells. Furthermore, NK cells use inhibitory receptors that recognize MHC class I; the absence of MHC class I leads to decreased inhibition of NK cells, but this is less relevant here than the impaired CTL function. The primary issue is the lack of MHC class I presentation, which is directly linked to low B2M. This impairs the immune response against intracellular pathogens like MAC.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **MHC Class I Assembly:** MHC class I molecules consist of a heavy chain (alpha chain) encoded by HLA-A, HLA-B, or HLA-C genes and a smaller protein called β2-microglobulin (B2M), encoded by a gene on chromosome 15. B2M is essential for the proper folding, stability, and transport of the MHC class I heavy chain to the cell surface. Without B2M, the MHC class I heavy chain is unstable and rapidly degraded in the endoplasmic reticulum.
- **CD8+ T Cell Function:** CD8+ T cells (CTLs) recognize peptide antigens presented by MHC class I molecules on infected or tumor cells. Upon recognition, CTLs kill the target cell.
- **β2-Microglobulin (B2M):** B2M is a small protein found on the surface of almost all nucleated cells. It is freely filtered by the glomerulus and is not reabsorbed by the renal tubules, meaning it is normally cleared by the kidneys. In CKD, especially end-stage renal disease requiring dialysis, B2M levels in the serum decrease significantly because it is lost during dialysis.
- **Consequences of Low B2M:** Low serum B2M leads to decreased MHC class I expression on the surface of various cells, including lymphocytes, macrophages, and epithelial cells. This impairs the ability of CD8+ T cells to recognize and eliminate infected cells. It also affects NK cell function (reduced inhibition), but the primary defect in this context is impaired CTL response. Patients with low B2M are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly viral infections (like CMV, EBV) and intracellular bacteria (like MAC).
- **MAC Infection:** *Mycobacterium avium* complex is an intracellular bacterium. Control of MAC infection relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, including the activation of macrophages by IFN-γ (produced by Th1 cells and NK cells) and the killing of infected cells by CD8+ CTLs. Impaired MHC class I presentation and CTL function, as seen in B2M deficiency, significantly increases susceptibility to disseminated MAC infection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by CD4+ T cells, impairing macrophage activation. While IFN-γ is crucial for macrophage activation against MAC, the vignette points specifically to a defect in MHC class I expression on CD8+ T cells and low B2M, not primarily a CD4+ T cell defect in IFN-γ production. Although the patient has low CD4+ cells, the specific finding of absent MHC class I on CD8+ cells and low B2M points to a different primary mechanism.
- (B) Impaired antigen presentation by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs). MHC class II molecules present antigens to CD4+ T cells. While impaired MHC class II presentation can lead to immunodeficiency, the key finding here is the lack of MHC class I expression on CD8+ T cells and low B2M, which directly affects MHC class I-restricted immunity (CD8+ T cells). MHC class II presentation is not the primary defect indicated by the vignette.
- (D) Defective production of interleukin-2 (IL-2) by CD4+ T cells, impairing T cell proliferation. IL-2 is essential for T cell proliferation and function. While IL-2 deficiency can cause immunodeficiency, the specific findings of low B2M and absent MHC class I on CD8+ T cells point directly to a defect in MHC class I assembly and presentation, not primarily IL-2 production.
- (E) Defective production of antibodies by B cells, leading to impaired opsonization and complement activation. Antibody deficiency (e.g., XLA, CVID) primarily affects humoral immunity and increases susceptibility to extracellular bacteria, especially encapsulated ones. While antibodies play a role in MAC infection, the primary defense against intracellular MAC relies on cell-mediated immunity (CTLs, macrophages). The vignette's focus on MHC class I deficiency and low B2M strongly implicates a defect in cell-mediated immunity, not humoral immunity.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | B2M Deficiency (Correct Answer) | IFN-γ Deficiency (Distractor A) | MHC Class II Deficiency (Distractor B) | IL-2 Deficiency (Distractor D) | Antibody Deficiency (Distractor E) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------ | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Low B2M, ↓ MHC Class I          | ↓ IFN-γ production             | ↓ MHC Class II expression          | ↓ IL-2 production            | ↓ Antibody production             |
| **Affected Cells**      | All nucleated cells (esp. T cells) | Macrophages, T cells          | APCs (DCs, Macrophages, B cells) | T cells                      | B cells                          |
| **Immune Arm**          | Cell-mediated (CD8+ T cells)     | Cell-mediated (Macrophage Act.) | Cell-mediated (CD4+ T cells)       | Cell-mediated (T cells)      | Humoral                          |
| **Key Susceptibility**  | Intracellular pathogens (MAC, CMV) | Intracellular pathogens (MAC, Listeria) | Extracellular bacteria, Fungi | Severe combined immunodeficiency | Encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*) |
| **Clinical Context**    | CKD on dialysis                 | Genetic defect, IFN-γ blockade | Bare lymphocyte syndrome          | Genetic defect                | Genetic defect, CVID              |
| **Vignette Clues**      | Low B2M, ↓ MHC I on CD8+ T cells | Low CD4+, MAC infection        | N/A                               | N/A                           | N/A                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Low serum β2-microglobulin, often seen in chronic kidney disease patients on dialysis, leads to impaired MHC class I molecule assembly and surface expression. This compromises CD8+ T cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC). Key differentials for opportunistic infections in immunocompromised hosts include defects in T cell function (CD4+ or CD8+), macrophage activation (IFN-γ), antibody production, or complement pathways. Always consider the specific pathogen and the nature of the immune defect suggested by the clinical and laboratory findings.

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 12 (antigen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of negative selection of T cells in the thymus, specifically the role of medullary thymic epithelial cells (mTECs) and the autoimmune regulator (AIRE) gene in presenting tissue-restricted antigens (TRAs) to developing T cells, preventing autoimmunity. (理解胸腺中T细胞的阴性选择过程，特别是髓质胸腺上皮细胞 (mTECs) 和自身免疫调节基因 (AIRE) 在呈现组织特异性抗原 (TRAs) 给发育中的 T 细胞中的作用，以防止自身免疫。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe mucocutaneous candidiasis, eczema, and failure to thrive. He has a history of multiple severe infections, including pneumonia and sepsis, requiring frequent hospitalizations. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from complications of a severe autoimmune disorder involving multiple endocrine glands. Immunological workup reveals markedly reduced numbers of peripheral CD4+ T cells and CD8+ T cells. Flow cytometry analysis of his T cells shows normal expression of CD3, CD4, and CD8, but absent expression of CD25. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *AIRE* gene. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the patient's susceptibility to both severe infections and autoimmune manifestations?

(A) Defective rearrangement of the T cell receptor (TCR) genes leading to non-functional T cells.
(B) Impaired migration of immature T cells from the bone marrow to the thymus.
(C) Failure of positive selection in the thymic cortex due to deficient MHC class I expression on cortical thymic epithelial cells (cTECs).
(D) Defective negative selection in the thymic medulla due to impaired presentation of tissue-restricted antigens (TRAs) by medullary thymic epithelial cells (mTECs).
(E) Aberrant expression of programmed cell death receptor 1 (PD-1) on developing T cells, leading to premature apoptosis.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent severe mucocutaneous candidiasis, eczema, failure to thrive, and multiple severe infections (pneumonia, sepsis). This constellation points towards a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a severe T-cell deficiency.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from severe autoimmune endocrine disorder. This suggests a genetic link involving autoimmunity.
- **Immunological Findings:** Markedly reduced peripheral CD4+ and CD8+ T cells indicate a T-cell deficiency. Normal CD3, CD4, CD8 expression suggests the cells are of T-cell lineage but are severely depleted. Absent CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) expression is unusual but can be seen in certain immunodeficiencies.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *AIRE* gene. This is the key piece of information. The *AIRE* gene encodes the autoimmune regulator protein.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining susceptibility to both infections (due to T-cell deficiency) and autoimmunity (suggested by family history and linked to the *AIRE* mutation).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **AIRE Function:** The AIRE protein is primarily expressed in medullary thymic epithelial cells (mTECs). Its function is crucial for inducing the expression of a wide range of tissue-restricted antigens (TRAs) – proteins normally expressed only in specific peripheral tissues (e.g., insulin from pancreatic beta cells, thyroglobulin from the thyroid).
- **Thymic Selection:** T cell development occurs in the thymus. Immature T cells (thymocytes) undergo positive selection in the cortex, where they must recognize self-MHC molecules presenting self-peptides with low affinity to survive. Thymocytes that fail positive selection die by apoptosis. Surviving thymocytes migrate to the medulla.
- **Negative Selection:** In the medulla, thymocytes undergo negative selection. mTECs, under the direction of AIRE, express TRAs. Thymocytes that bind strongly to these TRAs presented on MHC molecules are eliminated (deleted) by apoptosis. This process removes potentially autoreactive T cells that could attack peripheral tissues.
- **AIRE Deficiency (Autoimmune Polyendocrinopathy-Candidiasis-Ectodermal Dystrophy - APECED):** Mutations in the *AIRE* gene lead to a failure of mTECs to express TRAs. Consequently, autoreactive T cells specific for these TRAs escape negative selection in the thymus and mature. These autoreactive T cells then migrate to the periphery and attack target tissues, causing autoimmune diseases (e.g., endocrinopathies like hypoparathyroidism, adrenal insufficiency, type 1 diabetes; ectodermal dystrophy; chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis).
- **Link to Infections:** While AIRE deficiency primarily causes autoimmunity, the underlying defect in thymic selection can also lead to T-cell deficiency. The exact mechanism for T-cell deficiency in AIRE deficiency is complex and not fully understood, but it may involve defects in T-cell development or survival within the thymus, potentially related to the altered thymic microenvironment or impaired T-cell receptor repertoire development. The severe infections seen in the patient are consistent with this T-cell deficiency.
- **Connecting to the Vignette:** The patient has symptoms of both autoimmunity (suggested by family history and the nature of AIRE deficiency) and severe T-cell deficiency (recurrent infections, low T-cell counts). The *AIRE* gene mutation directly explains the failure of negative selection due to impaired presentation of TRAs by mTECs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective rearrangement of the T cell receptor (TCR) genes leading to non-functional T cells.** This describes conditions like severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to RAG1/2 deficiency or V(D)J recombination defects. While these cause T-cell deficiency and infections, they do not directly explain the autoimmune component linked to AIRE deficiency. TCR rearrangement defects typically lead to a complete absence or severe dysfunction of T cells, often presenting earlier and more severely than AIRE deficiency, and are not associated with autoimmunity in this specific manner.
- (B) **Impaired migration of immature T cells from the bone marrow to the thymus.** T cells develop in the thymus, not the bone marrow (B cells develop in the bone marrow). Impaired migration from the bone marrow would affect B-cell development, not T-cell development. This is incorrect.
- (C) **Failure of positive selection in the thymic cortex due to deficient MHC class I expression on cortical thymic epithelial cells (cTECs).** Failure of positive selection leads to a lack of mature T cells, particularly CD8+ T cells (which recognize peptides presented by MHC class I). This would cause severe T-cell deficiency. However, it does not explain the autoimmune component associated with AIRE deficiency. MHC class I deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type I) is a distinct condition. AIRE deficiency affects negative selection in the medulla, not positive selection in the cortex.
- (E) **Aberrant expression of programmed cell death receptor 1 (PD-1) on developing T cells, leading to premature apoptosis.** PD-1 is an inhibitory receptor expressed on mature T cells that regulates T-cell activation and exhaustion. While excessive PD-1 signaling can lead to T-cell dysfunction or apoptosis, it is not the primary mechanism underlying AIRE deficiency or the failure of negative selection. AIRE deficiency specifically affects the presentation of TRAs during negative selection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | AIRE Deficiency (APECED)                                  | RAG Deficiency (SCID)                                     | MHC Class I Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type I) | Positive Selection Failure (e.g., TAP1/2 deficiency) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Impaired TRA presentation by mTECs (negative selection) | Defective V(D)J recombination (TCR gene rearrangement)   | Deficient MHC Class I expression (positive selection)    | Impaired peptide loading onto MHC Class I (positive selection) |
| **Location of Defect**  | Thymic Medulla                                            | Bone Marrow (lymphoid progenitors)                         | Thymic Cortex (cTECs)                                   | Thymic Cortex (cTECs)                                |
| **Affected Cells**      | Primarily T cells (autoreactive), also T-cell numbers     | T cells, B cells (often)                                   | CD8+ T cells primarily                                   | CD8+ T cells primarily                                |
| **Key Manifestations**  | Autoimmunity (endocrine, ectodermal), Candidiasis, Infections | Severe infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, opportunistic) | Severe infections (viral, intracellular bacteria)        | Severe infections (viral, intracellular bacteria)        |
| **AIRE Gene**           | Mutated                                                   | Normal                                                    | Normal                                                   | Normal                                                |
| **Mechanism**           | Failure of negative selection                             | Failure of TCR gene rearrangement                          | Failure of positive selection                             | Failure of positive selection                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The *AIRE* gene is essential for negative selection in the thymic medulla by enabling mTECs to express tissue-restricted antigens (TRAs). Deficiency leads to autoimmunity (APECED) due to the escape of autoreactive T cells and can also cause T-cell deficiency, resulting in susceptibility to infections. Differentiate AIRE deficiency from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T-cell development, such as SCID (RAG deficiency) or defects in MHC expression or positive selection.

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 13 (binding)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of MHC class I and class II molecules in T cell development, specifically positive and negative selection in the thymus, and the consequences of defects in these molecules or their associated pathways. (理解 MHC I 类和 II 类分子在 T 细胞发育中的作用，特别是胸腺中的阳性选择和阴性选择，以及这些分子或相关通路缺陷的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe bacterial infections (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*) and opportunistic fungal infections (e.g., *Candida albicans*) starting shortly after birth. He has a history of failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a markedly reduced number of CD8+ T cells (approximately 5% of total T cells, normal range 30-50%) and a normal number of CD4+ T cells (approximately 35% of total T cells, normal range 30-50%). Further investigation reveals that his T cells express normal levels of the T cell receptor (TCR) beta chain but fail to express the CD8 alpha chain. MHC class I molecules are expressed normally on his cells. Which of the following cellular processes is most likely defective in this patient?

(A) Expression of MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(B) Positive selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymus.
(C) Negative selection of self-reactive CD8+ T cells in the thymus.
(D) Interaction between the TCR and MHC class I molecules during CD8+ T cell development.
(E) Expression of the CD3 complex on T cells.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial and opportunistic fungal infections:** Suggests a significant T cell immunodeficiency, as T cells are crucial for cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens (like fungi) and for helping B cells produce antibodies against encapsulated bacteria.
- **Onset shortly after birth:** Points towards a congenital immunodeficiency.
- **Failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly:** Common features of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or other severe T cell defects.
- **Flow cytometry: Markedly reduced CD8+ T cells, normal CD4+ T cells:** This specific pattern is the most crucial clue. It indicates a selective defect affecting CD8+ T cell development.
- **Normal TCR beta chain expression:** Rules out defects in the TCR gene rearrangement process itself for the beta chain.
- **Failure to express CD8 alpha chain:** This is the key finding. CD8 is a co-receptor primarily found on cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), which are CD8+ T cells.
- **Normal MHC class I expression:** Rules out defects in MHC class I expression as the primary cause of the CD8+ T cell deficiency. MHC class I is necessary for CD8+ T cell development.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Development in the Thymus:** T cell precursors migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus.
    - **Positive Selection (阳性选择):** Occurs in the thymic cortex. T cells whose TCRs can weakly bind to self-MHC molecules (either class I or class II) presented by thymic epithelial cells (TECs) receive survival signals. T cells that cannot bind MHC at all undergo apoptosis (death by neglect). This ensures that mature T cells are MHC-restricted.
        - CD4+ T cells are positively selected based on their ability to bind MHC class II.
        - CD8+ T cells are positively selected based on their ability to bind MHC class I.
    - **Negative Selection (阴性选择):** Occurs primarily in the thymic medulla. T cells that bind too strongly to self-antigens presented by MHC molecules on dendritic cells and macrophages undergo apoptosis (clonal deletion). This eliminates potentially autoreactive T cells.
- **CD8+ T Cell Development:** CD8+ T cells develop from TCR beta/CD4+CD8+ thymocytes. For a CD8+ T cell to survive positive selection, its TCR must recognize a peptide presented by MHC class I molecules on thymic epithelial cells. If the TCR cannot bind MHC class I, the cell dies. If it binds weakly, it survives and differentiates into a CD8+ T cell.
- **The Patient's Defect:** The patient has normal CD4+ T cells, indicating that positive selection for MHC class II recognition is intact. However, he has severely reduced CD8+ T cells. The fact that his T cells express the TCR beta chain but fail to express the CD8 alpha chain suggests a defect specifically affecting the pathway leading to CD8+ T cell maturation. The most likely explanation is a failure in positive selection for CD8+ T cells, meaning their TCRs cannot effectively bind to MHC class I molecules during development. This could be due to a defect in the TCR itself (specifically the alpha chain, which pairs with the beta chain to form the complete TCR), a defect in the signaling pathway downstream of TCR-MHC interaction, or a defect in the expression or function of MHC class I molecules (although the vignette states MHC class I expression is normal on his cells, it doesn't rule out a defect in the *interaction* itself or a defect in the thymocyte's ability to *recognize* MHC class I). Given the options, the most direct interpretation of the reduced CD8+ cells and normal CD4+ cells is a failure of the TCR-MHC class I interaction required for CD8+ T cell positive selection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Expression of MHC class II molecules on APCs:** Defects in MHC class II expression (e.g., MHC class II deficiency, also known as "bare lymphocyte syndrome type II") primarily affect CD4+ T cell development and function, leading to a deficiency of CD4+ T cells, not CD8+ T cells. This patient has normal CD4+ T cells.
- (B) **Positive selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymus:** Defects in positive selection for CD4+ T cells would lead to a deficiency of CD4+ T cells, as these cells require TCR recognition of MHC class II for survival. This patient has normal CD4+ T cells.
- (C) **Negative selection of self-reactive CD8+ T cells in the thymus:** Defects in negative selection typically lead to autoimmunity due to the survival of autoreactive T cells. While some severe defects in negative selection can cause immunodeficiency (e.g., AIRE deficiency), they don't typically cause a selective deficiency of CD8+ T cells with normal CD4+ T cells. AIRE deficiency often presents with autoimmune polyendocrinopathy-candidiasis-ectodermal dystrophy (APECED).
- (E) **Expression of the CD3 complex on T cells:** The CD3 complex is part of the TCR complex and is essential for signal transduction following TCR engagement. Defects in CD3 expression or function cause severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) because both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells require functional TCR signaling for development and survival. This patient has normal CD4+ T cells, making a general CD3 defect unlikely.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | MHC Class II Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type II) | MHC Class I Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type I) | AIRE Deficiency (APECED) | Patient's Condition (Selective CD8+ Deficiency) |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | MHC Class II gene mutation                               | MHC Class I gene mutation                               | AIRE gene mutation        | Defect in TCR-MHC I interaction/CD8 maturation |
| **CD4+ T Cells**            | Severely reduced/absent                                  | Normal                                                  | Normal/Reduced            | Normal                                         |
| **CD8+ T Cells**            | Normal                                                  | Severely reduced/absent                                  | Normal                    | Severely reduced                                |
| **Clinical Presentation**   | Recurrent bacterial/viral infections                     | Recurrent viral infections, increased cancer risk         | Autoimmunity, candidiasis | Recurrent bacterial/fungal infections           |
| **MHC Class I Expression**  | Normal                                                  | Absent/Reduced                                            | Normal                    | Normal (on cells)                               |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Absent/Reduced                                            | Normal                                                  | Normal                    | Normal (on APCs)                                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The selective deficiency of CD8+ T cells with normal CD4+ T cells points to a defect specifically affecting the positive selection process for CD8+ T cells, which requires interaction between the TCR and MHC class I molecules. Differentiating this from defects affecting MHC class II (affecting CD4+ cells) or general T cell development (like CD3 defects affecting both CD4+ and CD8+ cells) is crucial. Remember that positive selection ensures T cells are MHC-restricted, while negative selection eliminates self-reactive clones.

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 14 (formed)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of positive and negative selection of T cells in the thymus, specifically the role of MHC molecules in presenting self-peptides to developing thymocytes, leading to the maturation of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells and the elimination of self-reactive T cells (T cell tolerance).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy with a history of recurrent severe bacterial infections, including *Staphylococcus aureus* pneumonia and *Pseudomonas aeruginosa* sepsis, is evaluated. He has a history of failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a markedly reduced number of CD4+ T cells (T helper cells) and a normal number of CD8+ T cells (cytotoxic T cells). Furthermore, the remaining CD4+ T cells exhibit significantly reduced proliferative responses to mitogenic stimulation (e.g., PHA, ConA) and specific antigens. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *HLA-DRB1* gene, encoding the beta chain of MHC class II molecules.

Which of the following cellular processes is most likely impaired in this patient?

(A) Positive selection of CD8+ T cells in the thymus.
(B) Negative selection of CD8+ T cells in the thymus.
(C) Positive selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymus.
(D) Negative selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymus.
(E) Development of B cells in the bone marrow.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections:** Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity, particularly T helper cell function, which is crucial for activating macrophages and B cells to fight intracellular and encapsulated bacteria.
- **Failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea:** Common manifestations of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or related T cell defects.
- **Lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly:** Can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, including those affecting lymphocyte development or function.
- **Flow cytometry: Reduced CD4+ T cells, normal CD8+ T cells:** This specific pattern points towards a defect affecting the development or survival of CD4+ T cells.
- **Reduced proliferative response to mitogens/antigens:** Indicates impaired T cell function, consistent with a T cell deficiency.
- **Homozygous mutation in *HLA-DRB1* gene (MHC class II):** This is the critical clue. MHC class II molecules are essential for presenting antigens to CD4+ T cells. A defect in MHC class II expression will severely impact the development and function of CD4+ T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T cell development in the thymus:** Immature T cells (thymocytes) migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus.
- **Positive selection:** Occurs in the thymic cortex. Thymocytes expressing T cell receptors (TCRs) that can weakly bind to self-MHC molecules (either class I or class II) receive survival signals. Thymocytes that cannot bind MHC undergo apoptosis (death by neglect).
    - CD8+ T cells are positively selected by MHC class I molecules.
    - CD4+ T cells are positively selected by MHC class II molecules.
- **Negative selection:** Occurs primarily in the thymic medulla. Thymocytes that bind strongly to self-MHC molecules presenting self-peptides receive apoptotic signals, eliminating potentially autoreactive T cells. This process ensures central tolerance.
    - Both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells undergo negative selection.
- **MHC Class II and CD4+ T cells:** MHC class II molecules present processed exogenous antigens (e.g., from bacteria) to CD4+ T helper cells. The interaction between the TCR on the CD4+ T cell and the peptide-MHC class II complex, along with co-stimulatory signals, is crucial for T cell activation.
- **Impact of *HLA-DRB1* mutation:** A homozygous mutation leading to a lack of functional MHC class II molecules prevents CD4+ T cells from undergoing successful positive selection in the thymus. Thymocytes expressing TCRs specific for MHC class II cannot receive the necessary survival signals and undergo apoptosis. This results in a severe deficiency of mature CD4+ T cells in the periphery. While MHC class II is also involved in antigen presentation to B cells, the primary defect described here (reduced CD4+ T cells) directly relates to T cell development. The normal number of CD8+ T cells indicates that MHC class I-mediated positive selection is intact.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Positive selection of CD8+ T cells in the thymus:** This process depends on MHC class I molecules. The patient has a defect in MHC class II, not class I. CD8+ T cell numbers are normal, indicating this process is functional. Therefore, (A) is incorrect.
- (B) **Negative selection of CD8+ T cells in the thymus:** This process involves MHC class I molecules presenting self-peptides to CD8+ T cells. The patient's defect is in MHC class II, and CD8+ T cell numbers are normal, suggesting negative selection of CD8+ cells is largely intact. Therefore, (B) is incorrect.
- (D) **Negative selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymus:** While negative selection is crucial for eliminating self-reactive CD4+ T cells, the primary defect caused by the lack of MHC class II is the failure of *positive* selection. Without MHC class II, CD4+ T cells cannot be positively selected, leading to their absence even before negative selection occurs. While some self-reactive CD4+ cells might escape positive selection if MHC II were present, the profound deficiency is due to the lack of positive selection signals. Therefore, (D) is less likely than (C) as the primary cause of the CD4+ deficiency.
- (E) **Development of B cells in the bone marrow:** B cell development occurs in the bone marrow and is largely independent of MHC class II expression, although MHC class II is required for T-dependent B cell activation later. The patient's primary defect is a severe reduction in CD4+ T cells, not B cells. Therefore, (E) is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | MHC Class II Deficiency (e.g., Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome) | MHC Class I Deficiency (e.g., TAP1/2 deficiency) | DiGeorge Syndrome (Thymic Hypoplasia) | SCID (e.g., ADA deficiency) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Lack of MHC Class II expression                          | Impaired peptide transport to ER/Golgi                | Thymic aplasia/hypoplasia            | Various genetic defects   |
| **CD4+ T cells**        | Severely reduced/absent                                  | Normal                                               | Severely reduced/absent             | Severely reduced/absent   |
| **CD8+ T cells**        | Normal                                                   | Severely reduced/absent                               | Severely reduced/absent             | Severely reduced/absent   |
| **B cells**             | Normal number, impaired T-dependent activation            | Normal                                               | Normal                              | Normal/Abnormal           |
| **NK cells**            | Normal                                                   | Normal                                               | Normal                              | Normal                    |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Severe bacterial infections, failure to thrive           | Recurrent viral infections, normal bacterial response | Recurrent infections, hypocalcemia   | Severe recurrent infections |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Impaired CD4+ T cell positive selection                  | Impaired CD8+ T cell positive selection                | Impaired T cell maturation          | Impaired lymphocyte development/function |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The lack of MHC class II molecules prevents positive selection of CD4+ T cells in the thymus, leading to a severe deficiency of these cells and impaired cell-mediated immunity. This condition is known as Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome. Differentiating this from defects affecting MHC class I (affecting CD8+ T cells) or overall thymic development (affecting both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells) is crucial. Remember that positive selection requires TCR interaction with self-MHC, while negative selection eliminates strongly self-reactive cells.

<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 15 (between)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of V(D)J recombination, including the role of RAG enzymes, TdT, and the mechanisms that ensure diversity and prevent autoimmunity during lymphocyte development. Specifically, testing the understanding of the difference between V(D)J recombination and somatic hypermutation, and the consequences of defects in these processes. (理解 V(D)J 重组的过程，包括 RAG 酶、TdT 的作用，以及确保多样性和防止自身免疫的机制。具体测试对 V(D)J 重组和体细胞超突变之间差异的理解，以及这些过程中缺陷的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting shortly after his maternal antibodies waned at 6 months of age. He has a history of multiple episodes of severe varicella infection and chronic diarrhea. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died from lymphoma at age 30. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and splenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes shows markedly reduced numbers of both B cells and T cells. Further investigation reveals that his B cells are unable to produce antibodies against new antigens, including vaccine antigens, and his T cells exhibit impaired proliferative responses to mitogenic stimuli. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *RAG2* gene.

Which of the following mechanisms best explains the patient's inability to mount an effective adaptive immune response?

(A) Failure of somatic hypermutation, leading to the production of low-affinity antibodies.
(B) Defective class switch recombination, resulting in the inability to produce IgG, IgA, and IgE isotypes.
(C) Impaired V(D)J recombination, preventing the generation of diverse T cell receptor (TCR) and B cell receptor (BCR) gene arrangements.
(D) Aberrant expression of activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID), causing excessive somatic hypermutation and class switch recombination.
(E) Defective TdT activity, leading to the accumulation of non-functional, non-templated P-nucleotide additions at V(D)J junctions.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent infections (sinopulmonary, varicella, diarrhea) starting after 6 months:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting both humoral (B cell) and cellular (T cell) immunity, as maternal antibodies wane.
- **Severe varicella:** Indicates impaired cell-mediated immunity (T cells), as varicella-zoster virus (VZV) is typically controlled by T cells.
- **Family history of lymphoma:** Suggests a potential genetic predisposition or link to lymphocyte development/function defects.
- **Lymphadenopathy and splenomegaly:** Common findings in immunodeficiencies, often due to chronic infections or abnormal lymphocyte proliferation/accumulation.
- **Flow cytometry: Reduced B and T cells:** Points to a defect early in lymphocyte development, affecting both lineages.
- **Inability to produce antibodies against new antigens:** Confirms a severe B cell defect, likely preventing the generation of functional BCRs.
- **Impaired T cell proliferation:** Confirms a severe T cell defect, likely preventing the generation of functional TCRs.
- **Homozygous mutation in *RAG2* gene:** This is the key genetic finding. RAG1 and RAG2 enzymes are essential for V(D)J recombination.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Lymphocyte Development:** B and T lymphocytes develop from hematopoietic stem cells in the bone marrow (B cells) and thymus (T cells). A crucial step is the rearrangement of V(D)J gene segments to create unique antigen receptors (BCR and TCR).
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This process is initiated by the RAG1/RAG2 enzyme complex, which introduces double-strand breaks at specific recombination signal sequences (RSSs) flanking the V, D, and J gene segments. These breaks are then processed by non-homologous end joining (NHEJ) machinery, bringing the segments together to form a functional gene.
- **TdT (Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase):** This enzyme is active during V(D)J recombination, particularly in T cells, and adds random non-templated P-nucleotides (N-nucleotides) at the junctions between V, D, and J segments. This process contributes significantly to the diversity of the TCR and BCR repertoire.
- **Role of RAG2:** RAG2 is a critical component of the RAG complex. Mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* lead to Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) due to the inability to perform V(D)J recombination. This results in a profound lack of mature, functional B and T cells.
- **Somatic Hypermutation (SHM):** This process occurs *after* B cell activation in germinal centers. It involves introducing point mutations into the variable regions of the rearranged BCR gene, driven by AID and other enzymes. SHM increases the affinity of antibodies for their antigen. It does *not* occur during initial lymphocyte development.
- **Class Switch Recombination (CSR):** This process also occurs *after* B cell activation in germinal centers. It involves changing the constant region of the heavy chain (e.g., from IgM to IgG, IgA, or IgE), mediated by AID and switch regions. CSR changes the effector function of the antibody but does not affect antigen specificity. It does *not* occur during initial lymphocyte development.
- **AID (Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase):** This enzyme is essential for both SHM and CSR. Aberrant expression can lead to hyper-IgM syndrome (if deficient) or B cell lymphomas (if hyperactive or misregulated).

In this case, the *RAG2* mutation prevents the initial V(D)J recombination necessary for generating diverse BCR and TCR genes. This explains the lack of mature B and T cells, the inability to produce antibodies, and the impaired T cell function, leading to the clinical presentation of SCID.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Failure of somatic hypermutation:** SHM occurs *after* initial B cell activation and rearrangement, not during development. A defect in SHM would lead to low-affinity antibodies but would not prevent the initial generation of B cells or their ability to produce *some* antibody (likely IgM). The patient has a profound lack of B cells and inability to make antibodies to *new* antigens, indicating a developmental defect, not a post-activation defect like SHM.
- (B) **Defective class switch recombination:** CSR also occurs *after* B cell activation. A defect in CSR leads to hyper-IgM syndrome, where B cells can produce IgM but not other isotypes. Patients typically have normal or near-normal numbers of B cells and can mount responses to T-dependent antigens, although antibody function is limited. This patient has severely reduced B cell numbers and a complete inability to produce antibodies, inconsistent with a CSR defect.
- (D) **Aberrant expression of activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID):** AID is involved in SHM and CSR. Deficiency leads to hyper-IgM syndrome. Excess or aberrant activity is associated with B cell lymphomas or other hypermutator syndromes. Neither scenario explains the profound lack of B and T cells seen in this patient. The *RAG2* mutation directly affects the fundamental process of V(D)J recombination, which is upstream of AID function.
- (E) **Defective TdT activity:** TdT adds N-nucleotides at V(D)J junctions, contributing to diversity, especially in T cells. Deficiency in TdT leads to a lack of N-nucleotides, which can impair T cell receptor signaling and function, but it does not abolish V(D)J recombination itself. Patients with TdT deficiency still have functional B and T cells, although T cell function may be subtly impaired. The patient's presentation with near absence of B and T cells points to a more fundamental defect in the recombination process itself, caused by the RAG2 mutation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | RAG2 Deficiency (Correct Answer) | Somatic Hypermutation Defect | Class Switch Recombination Defect (Hyper-IgM) | AID Deficiency (Hyper-IgM) | TdT Deficiency |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | V(D)J Recombination              | Post-activation V region mutation | Post-activation Isotype switching              | Post-activation SHM & CSR   | N-nucleotide addition |
| **Timing**              | Lymphocyte Development           | Germinal Center (Post-activation) | Germinal Center (Post-activation)                | Germinal Center             | Development/Activation |
| **Affected Cells**      | B & T cells                      | B cells                         | B cells                                         | B cells                     | T cells (primarily) |
| **B Cell Numbers**      | Severely Reduced/Absent           | Normal/Near Normal              | Normal/Near Normal                               | Normal/Near Normal          | Normal          |
| **T Cell Numbers**      | Severely Reduced/Absent           | Normal                          | Normal                                           | Normal                      | Normal          |
| **Antibody Production** | Absent                           | Low Affinity IgM (some)         | IgM only (no IgG, IgA, IgE)                       | IgM only                    | Impaired T cell help |
| **Clinical Picture**    | SCID (Severe Combined Immunodeficiency) | Normal Immunity (mild issues) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (recurrent infections)       | Hyper-IgM Syndrome          | Subtle T cell defects |
| **Gene Involved**       | *RAG2*                            | *NA*                            | *NA*                                             | *AID*                       | *TdT*           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The inability of RAG enzymes (RAG1/RAG2) to initiate V(D)J recombination leads to Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) due to the lack of functional T and B cell receptors. This contrasts with defects in post-activation processes like somatic hypermutation (SHM) or class switch recombination (CSR), which affect antibody affinity or isotype but do not prevent initial lymphocyte development or antibody production. Remember that RAG deficiency affects both B and T cells, while defects in AID affect only B cells (leading to Hyper-IgM syndrome). TdT deficiency primarily affects T cell function.
