<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 31 (present)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of positive and negative selection of T cells in the thymus, specifically the role of MHC molecules in presenting self-peptides to developing thymocytes, and the consequences of defects in this process, leading to autoimmunity or immunodeficiency. (理解胸腺中T细胞的正向和负向选择过程，特别是MHC分子在向发育中的胸腺细胞呈递自身肽中的作用，以及该过程中缺陷的后果，导致自身免疫或免疫缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and chronic diarrhea, starting shortly after birth. He has a history of failure to thrive and eczema. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from complications of a severe, disseminated fungal infection. Physical examination reveals lymphopenia and hepatosplenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes shows markedly reduced numbers of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but normal numbers of B cells and NK cells. T-cell receptor (TCR) repertoire analysis reveals a severely limited diversity of TCRs. Further investigation reveals that his T cells are unable to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation (e.g., PHA) or allogeneic cells, but they do respond to strong, non-specific stimuli like PMA/ionomycin. MHC class I and class II expression on his cells is normal. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *CD3δ* gene, which is essential for the formation of the pre-TCR complex required for early T cell development.

Which of the following cellular processes is most likely defective in this patient?

(A) Positive selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs specific for self-MHC.
(B) Negative selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs with high affinity for self-antigens presented on cortical thymic epithelial cells (cTECs).
(C) Positive selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs specific for self-MHC, leading to differentiation into CD4+ or CD8+ single-positive cells.
(D) Negative selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs with high affinity for self-antigens presented on medullary thymic epithelial cells (mTECs).
(E) Migration of mature T cells from the thymus to the peripheral lymphoid organs.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent severe infections, failure to thrive, eczema:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency, likely involving T cells, given the severity and breadth of infections (bacterial, fungal, viral implied by pneumonia/diarrhea). Eczema can be seen in some immunodeficiencies like Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome or hyper-IgE syndrome, but the primary defect here points towards T-cell deficiency.
*   **Early onset (shortly after birth):** Points towards a congenital immunodeficiency.
*   **Family history (maternal uncle with disseminated fungal infection):** Suggests an X-linked or autosomal recessive inheritance pattern. Disseminated fungal infections are characteristic of severe T-cell defects.
*   **Lymphopenia, hepatosplenomegaly:** Common findings in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID).
*   **Flow cytometry: Reduced CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, normal B cells and NK cells:** This pattern is classic for SCID, specifically affecting T-cell development.
*   **TCR repertoire analysis: Severely limited diversity:** Indicates a block in T-cell development, preventing the generation of a diverse range of T cells.
*   **T cells unable to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation (PHA) or allogeneic cells:** PHA stimulates T cells non-specifically, and allogeneic cells stimulate T cells via MHC differences. Failure to respond indicates a fundamental defect in T-cell function or development. Response to PMA/ionomycin (bypassing TCR signaling) suggests the cells are viable but lack functional TCR signaling pathways.
*   **Normal MHC class I and II expression:** Rules out defects in antigen presentation itself.
*   ***CD3δ* gene mutation:** CD3δ is part of the CD3 complex, which is crucial for TCR signaling. More importantly, the CD3 complex (specifically CD3ε, CD3δ, CD3γ, and CD3ζ chains) associates with the TCRαβ heterodimer to form the pre-TCR complex. The pre-TCR complex signals through the Igα/Igβ heterodimer (which acts like a CD3 complex) and is essential for the survival and proliferation of double-negative (DN) thymocytes transitioning to double-positive (DP) stage, and subsequently for positive selection. A mutation in *CD3δ* disrupts pre-TCR signaling.

*   **Connecting the dots:** The clinical picture (early-onset SCID with T-cell lymphopenia and limited TCR diversity) combined with the genetic defect (*CD3δ* mutation) points to a failure in early T-cell development, specifically the transition from DN to DP stage and the subsequent positive selection process. The pre-TCR complex is essential for this transition and signaling.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **T Cell Development in the Thymus:** T cell precursors (hematopoietic stem cells) migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus.
    *   **Double-Negative (DN) Stage:** Thymocytes lack both CD4 and CD8 (CD4-CD8-). They undergo proliferation and rearrangement of the TCRβ, γ, and δ genes. Successful rearrangement of TCRβ leads to the expression of a pre-TCR complex (TCRβ chain + invariant pre-Tα chain + CD3 complex).
    *   **Pre-TCR Signaling:** The pre-TCR complex signals through the Igα/Igβ heterodimer, promoting survival, proliferation, and arrest of β-selection. This allows the cell to rearrange the TCRα locus.
    *   **Double-Positive (DP) Stage:** Thymocytes express both CD4 and CD8 (CD4+CD8+). They undergo positive and negative selection.
    *   **Positive Selection:** Occurs in the thymic cortex. DP thymocytes interact with cortical thymic epithelial cells (cTECs), which express MHC class I and class II molecules presenting self-peptides. Thymocytes whose TCRs can bind to self-MHC with *low to intermediate affinity* receive survival signals (mediated by CD4/CD8 co-receptors interacting with MHC class II/I, respectively, and the TCR interacting with the peptide-MHC complex). This ensures that mature T cells can recognize self-MHC, which is necessary for antigen presentation in the periphery. Thymocytes that cannot bind self-MHC undergo apoptosis (death by neglect).
    *   **Lineage Commitment:** After positive selection, thymocytes downregulate either CD4 or CD8, becoming CD4+ single-positive (SP) or CD8+ SP cells, based on the strength of TCR signaling and co-receptor interaction (CD4/MHCII vs CD8/MHC I).
    *   **Negative Selection:** Occurs primarily in the thymic medulla, but also in the cortex. SP thymocytes interact with medullary thymic epithelial cells (mTECs) and dendritic cells, which present a wide array of self-antigens (including tissue-specific antigens via AIRE expression in mTECs). Thymocytes whose TCRs bind to self-MHC with *high affinity* receive apoptotic signals, eliminating potentially autoreactive T cells.
    *   **Export:** Mature, positively selected, negatively selected SP T cells (CD4+ or CD8+) exit the thymus and migrate to peripheral lymphoid organs.

*   **Impact of *CD3δ* Mutation:** The CD3 complex is essential for TCR signaling. A mutation in *CD3δ* disrupts the formation or function of the pre-TCR complex. This leads to:
    *   Failure of DN thymocytes to successfully transition to the DP stage.
    *   Severe reduction in the number of thymocytes reaching the DP stage.
    *   Consequently, a profound block in both positive and negative selection processes, as these occur in the DP stage.
    *   Resulting in a severe deficiency of mature CD4+ and CD8+ T cells in the periphery (SCID).
    *   The limited TCR diversity reflects the failure of V(D)J recombination and selection processes to occur effectively.

*   **Relating to Options:**
    *   (A) Positive selection requires thymocytes to reach the DP stage and express functional TCRs. The *CD3δ* mutation prevents this, so positive selection is severely impaired, but the *primary* defect is the inability to *reach* the stage where positive selection occurs.
    *   (B) Negative selection also occurs in the DP stage. Similar to positive selection, it cannot occur effectively if thymocytes fail to reach or progress through the DP stage due to the pre-TCR defect.
    *   (C) Positive selection *is* defective because the cells cannot properly transition to the DP stage or form functional TCR complexes needed for signaling during positive selection. The pre-TCR signaling defect prevents the expansion and survival required for positive selection and subsequent lineage commitment. This option accurately describes a key consequence of the underlying defect.
    *   (D) Negative selection is also defective for the same reasons as positive selection.
    *   (E) Migration occurs *after* successful selection. The primary defect is in the selection process itself, preventing the generation of mature T cells that can migrate.

*   **Conclusion:** The *CD3δ* mutation causes a block in early T-cell development, specifically preventing the transition from DN to DP stage and the formation of a functional pre-TCR complex. This fundamentally disrupts both positive and negative selection processes. Option (C) describes the positive selection process, which is critically dependent on the successful transition to the DP stage and functional TCR signaling, both of which are impaired by the *CD3δ* mutation. While negative selection is also impaired, the inability to undergo positive selection due to the pre-TCR defect is the most direct consequence of the mutation described. The question asks for the *most likely defective* process, and the failure to undergo positive selection (and subsequent lineage commitment) is the core issue stemming from the inability to form a functional pre-TCR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Positive selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs specific for self-MHC. While positive selection is indeed defective in this patient, the *underlying* defect (mutation in *CD3δ*) prevents the thymocytes from even reaching the DP stage where positive selection occurs. The pre-TCR is required for the transition to DP. Therefore, the defect is more fundamental than just positive selection itself; it's the inability to *undergo* positive selection due to a prior developmental block.
- (B): Negative selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs with high affinity for self-antigens presented on cTECs. Negative selection occurs later in development (primarily medulla, but also cortex) and requires thymocytes to be in the SP stage. Since the patient's T cells fail to develop beyond the DN stage or very early DP stage, negative selection cannot occur effectively. This is a consequence of the primary defect, not the defect itself. Also, negative selection primarily involves mTECs and DCs, not just cTECs.
- (D): Negative selection of thymocytes expressing TCRs with high affinity for self-antigens presented on mTECs. Similar to (B), negative selection is impaired as a consequence of the developmental block caused by the *CD3δ* mutation, but it is not the primary defective process itself. The cells don't reach the stage where they can be negatively selected.
- (E): Migration of mature T cells from the thymus to the peripheral lymphoid organs. Mature T cells cannot migrate because they are not produced in sufficient numbers due to the block in T-cell development and selection within the thymus. This is a downstream consequence of the defective selection processes, not the primary defect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Patient (CD3δ mutation)                                  | DiGeorge Syndrome (TBX1 mutation)                               | X-linked SCID (IL2RG mutation)                                  | Autoimmune Lymphoproliferative Syndrome (FAS/FASL mutation) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Pre-TCR signaling, early T-cell development block        | Thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, impaired T-cell development          | Defective IL-2 signaling, impaired T-cell proliferation/survival | Defective apoptosis of lymphocytes                             |
| **T Cell Development**  | Blocked at DN/early DP stage                             | Reduced thymic environment, impaired positive/negative selection | Impaired proliferation/survival after TCR stimulation           | Increased lymphocyte numbers, accumulation of autoreactive cells |
| **T Cell Numbers**      | Severely reduced CD4+ and CD8+ T cells (SCID)           | Reduced CD4+ and CD8+ T cells (variable severity)              | Severely reduced CD4+ T cells, variable CD8+ T cells (SCID)     | Normal or increased T cells                                   |
| **B Cell Numbers**      | Normal                                                   | Normal                                                          | Normal                                                          | Normal                                                       |
| **NK Cell Numbers**     | Normal                                                   | Normal                                                          | Normal                                                          | Normal                                                       |
| **TCR Diversity**       | Severely limited                                         | Reduced                                                         | Severely limited                                                 | Normal                                                       |
| **Key Feature**         | Defective pre-TCR signaling                             | Absent/hypoplastic thymus                                       | Defective cytokine signaling                                     | Failure of apoptosis, lymphadenopathy, autoimmunity           |
| **Positive Selection**  | Defective (cannot reach DP stage effectively)             | Defective (due to lack of thymic environment)                  | Defective (due to impaired proliferation/survival)              | Normal (but ineffective negative selection)                   |
| **Negative Selection**  | Defective (cannot reach SP stage effectively)             | Defective (due to lack of thymic environment)                  | Defective (due to impaired proliferation/survival)              | Defective (leading to accumulation of autoreactive cells)      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The *CD3δ* mutation disrupts pre-TCR signaling, blocking T-cell development at the DN to DP transition stage. This leads to a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) characterized by profound T-cell lymphopenia and impaired positive and negative selection. Key differentials for SCID include IL2RG mutations (X-linked SCID) and RAG mutations (affecting V(D)J recombination), while DiGeorge syndrome involves thymic hypoplasia. Understanding the specific stage at which T-cell development is blocked is crucial for diagnosing these conditions.

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 32 (molecule, B2M)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of β2-microglobulin (B2M) in MHC class I molecule assembly and its importance in antigen presentation, particularly in the context of immune cell function and potential deficiencies. (理解β2-微球蛋白(B2M)在MHC I类分子组装中的作用及其在抗原呈递中的重要性，特别是在免疫细胞功能和潜在缺陷的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, chronic diarrhea, and failure to thrive since infancy. He has a history of multiple hospitalizations for pneumonia and sinusitis. Physical examination reveals cachexia, lymphadenopathy, and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory tests show markedly reduced levels of CD8+ T cells and NK cells. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a lack of expression of MHC class I molecules on the surface of most lymphocytes, monocytes, and granulocytes. His serum β2-microglobulin levels are undetectable. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *B2M* gene. Which of the following best describes the underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Impaired V(D)J recombination leading to a lack of functional T cell receptors (TCRs) and B cell receptors (BCRs).
(B) Defective processing and presentation of endogenous antigens by MHC class I molecules, leading to impaired cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) function and NK cell activation.
(C) Failure of positive selection of T cells in the thymus due to a lack of MHC class II molecules, resulting in a deficiency of CD4+ T cells.
(D) Defective complement activation pathway preventing opsonization and lysis of encapsulated bacteria.
(E) Impaired immunoglobulin class switching due to a deficiency in CD40 ligand (CD40L), leading to hyper-IgM syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, chronic diarrhea, failure to thrive:** Suggests a significant immunodeficiency, likely affecting cell-mediated immunity and potentially humoral immunity.
- **Reduced CD8+ T cells and NK cells:** Points towards a defect affecting these specific lymphocyte populations. NK cells, while not directly MHC-restricted in their killing, rely on MHC class I expression on target cells for inhibition (missing-self hypothesis) and can be indirectly affected by defects impacting MHC class I expression. CD8+ T cells (CTLs) are critically dependent on MHC class I for antigen recognition.
- **Lack of MHC class I expression on lymphocytes, monocytes, granulocytes:** This is a crucial finding. MHC class I molecules are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells and are essential for presenting endogenous antigens (e.g., viral proteins, tumor antigens) to CD8+ T cells. Their absence severely impairs CTL function.
- **Undetectable serum β2-microglobulin (B2M):** B2M is an essential component of the MHC class I heavy chain. It forms a non-covalently bound complex with the heavy chain in the endoplasmic reticulum, facilitating proper folding, assembly, and transport of the complete MHC class I molecule to the cell surface. Lack of B2M prevents stable MHC class I formation.
- **Homozygous *B2M* gene mutation:** Confirms a genetic defect directly affecting B2M production.
- **Question asks for the underlying immunological defect:** The combination of absent B2M, absent MHC class I expression, and reduced CD8+ T cells/NK cells strongly points to a defect in MHC class I-mediated antigen presentation and its consequences.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **MHC Class I Pathway:** Endogenous antigens (e.g., viral proteins synthesized in the cytoplasm) are degraded into peptides by the proteasome. These peptides are transported into the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) via TAP (Transporter associated with Antigen Processing). In the ER, peptides bind to MHC class I heavy chains, which are associated with β2-microglobulin (B2M). The stable MHC class I-peptide complex is then transported to the cell surface for presentation to CD8+ T cells (CTLs).
- **Role of B2M:** B2M is a small, ubiquitous protein that is non-covalently associated with the α3 domain of the MHC class I heavy chain. It is essential for the proper folding, stability, and trafficking of MHC class I molecules from the ER to the cell surface. Without B2M, MHC class I heavy chains are unstable, misfolded, and rapidly degraded in the proteasome, preventing their expression on the cell surface.
- **Consequences of MHC Class I Deficiency:**
    - **Impaired CTL function:** CD8+ T cells cannot recognize and kill infected or cancerous cells presenting endogenous antigens via MHC class I. This leads to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens (viruses, intracellular bacteria) and potentially increased risk of certain cancers.
    - **NK cell dysregulation:** NK cells possess inhibitory receptors (e.g., KIRs) that recognize MHC class I molecules on target cells. The absence of MHC class I ("missing-self") leads to reduced inhibition of NK cells, potentially causing them to kill healthy cells expressing low levels of MHC I or leading to other dysfunctions. However, the primary defect here is the lack of MHC I presentation to CTLs. The reduction in NK cells observed in the vignette might be secondary or related to the overall immune dysregulation.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Patients with severe MHC class I deficiency (like B2M deficiency) typically present early in life with severe, recurrent infections, particularly viral infections, and often have failure to thrive.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Impaired V(D)J recombination:** This leads to defects in the generation of diverse TCRs and BCRs, causing severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). While SCID presents with severe infections, the specific findings of absent MHC class I and reduced CD8+ T cells/NK cells are not characteristic of V(D)J recombination defects. V(D)J recombination defects affect the *diversity* of lymphocyte receptors, not the *expression* of MHC molecules.
- (C) **Failure of positive selection of T cells in the thymus due to a lack of MHC class II molecules:** This describes MHC class II deficiency (e.g., Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome type II). MHC class II is crucial for presenting exogenous antigens to CD4+ T cells. Deficiency leads to a lack of CD4+ T cells and impaired humoral immunity (due to lack of T cell help for B cells). The vignette describes absent MHC class I and reduced CD8+ T cells/NK cells, not MHC class II and CD4+ T cells.
- (D) **Defective complement activation pathway:** Complement deficiencies typically lead to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Neisseria*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). While infections are common, the specific pattern of reduced CD8+ T cells/NK cells and absent MHC class I expression is not explained by a complement defect.
- (E) **Impaired immunoglobulin class switching due to a deficiency in CD40 ligand (CD40L):** This describes X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome. CD40L on activated T cells interacts with CD40 on B cells, which is essential for class switching and memory B cell formation. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE, leading to susceptibility to bacterial and opportunistic infections. This does not explain the absent MHC class I or reduced CD8+ T cells/NK cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | B2M Deficiency (Correct) | V(D)J Recombination Defect (SCID) | MHC Class II Deficiency | Complement Deficiency | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (CD40L Def.) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :-------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | B2M production/MHC I assembly | TCR/BCR gene rearrangement       | MHC Class II expression | Complement proteins   | CD40L expression               |
| **MHC Class I Expression** | Absent                  | Normal                            | Normal                 | Normal               | Normal                        |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Normal                  | Normal                            | Absent                 | Normal               | Normal                        |
| **CD8+ T Cells**        | Reduced                 | Normal/Reduced (depending on type) | Normal                 | Normal               | Normal                        |
| **CD4+ T Cells**        | Normal                  | Normal/Reduced (depending on type) | Absent                 | Normal               | Normal                        |
| **NK Cells**            | Reduced                 | Normal/Reduced (depending on type) | Normal                 | Normal               | Normal                        |
| **Key Infections**      | Viral, Intracellular     | Broad (Viral, Bacterial, Fungal)  | Bacterial, Opportunistic | Encapsulated Bacteria | Opportunistic (Pneumocystis)   |
| **Genetic Basis**       | *B2M* mutation           | Various (e.g., *RAG1/2*, *JAK3*)  | *HLA-DR* mutations     | Various              | *CD40LG* mutation              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
β2-microglobulin is essential for MHC class I molecule stability and surface expression. Its deficiency leads to impaired antigen presentation to CD8+ T cells and affects NK cell regulation, resulting in severe susceptibility to intracellular pathogens. Differentiate this from MHC class II deficiency (affecting CD4+ T cells), complement deficiency (affecting opsonization/lysis), V(D)J recombination defects (affecting receptor diversity), and CD40L deficiency (affecting class switching).

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 33 (conformation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of V(D)J recombination, somatic hypermutation, and class switch recombination in B cell development and activation, specifically the role of activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) and its consequences, including potential errors leading to B cell lymphomas. (理解B细胞发育和激活过程中V(D)J重组、体细胞高变异和类别转换重组的过程，特别是激活诱导胞嘧啶脱氨酶(AID)的作用及其后果，包括可能导致B细胞淋巴瘤的错误。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) treated with fludarabine and cyclophosphamide 5 years ago presents with rapidly progressive B symptoms (fever, night sweats, weight loss) and generalized lymphadenopathy. He was previously in remission. A lymph node biopsy reveals a diffuse proliferation of large, atypical B lymphocytes. Flow cytometry shows the cells are CD19+, CD20+, CD5+, CD10-, CD23+, and surface immunoglobulin (sIg) positive, but with restricted light chain expression (kappa only). Cytogenetic analysis reveals a translocation t(14;18)(q32;q21). Further molecular studies show high levels of somatic hypermutation in the variable region genes of the immunoglobulin heavy chain and frequent switch regions in the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus. Which of the following molecular events is most likely responsible for the development of this aggressive lymphoma in this patient?

(A) Failure of RAG1/RAG2 complex to initiate V(D)J recombination during B cell development in the bone marrow.
(B) Aberrant expression of TdT (terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase) leading to P-nucleotide addition at class switch recombination sites.
(C) Dysregulation of activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) activity, leading to aberrant somatic hypermutation and class switch recombination events.
(D) Defective function of the DNA mismatch repair (MMR) system, resulting in increased rates of point mutations during V(D)J recombination.
(E) Inactivation of the PAX5 transcription factor, preventing the normal progression of B cell maturation beyond the pro-B cell stage.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient History:** 62-year-old male with a history of CLL, previously treated with chemotherapy (fludarabine, cyclophosphamide). This history is significant because CLL is a B-cell malignancy, and chemotherapy can induce secondary malignancies, often related to the original lineage or due to genomic instability.
- **Presentation:** Rapidly progressive B symptoms and generalized lymphadenopathy suggest a relapse or transformation of the original CLL, or the development of a new, aggressive lymphoma.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse proliferation of large, atypical B lymphocytes indicates a high-grade lymphoma.
- **Flow Cytometry:** CD19+, CD20+, CD5+, CD10-, CD23+, sIg+ (kappa only) is characteristic of CLL/Small Lymphocytic Lymphoma (SLL). The presence of large, atypical cells suggests transformation to a more aggressive lymphoma, such as Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma (DLBCL), often Richter's transformation in the context of CLL. The restricted light chain expression (kappa only) is typical for a clonal B-cell population.
- **Cytogenetics:** t(14;18)(q32;q21) is a classic translocation associated with follicular lymphoma, involving the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus (IGH, 14q32) and the BCL2 gene (18q21). While typically seen in follicular lymphoma, it can occur in other B-cell lymphomas, including those arising from CLL.
- **Molecular Studies:** High levels of somatic hypermutation (SHM) in the V region genes and frequent switch regions in the IgH locus are hallmarks of B cell activation and affinity maturation processes, mediated by AID. These processes normally occur in germinal centers. Their presence in a transformed lymphoma suggests ongoing or dysregulated B cell activation processes.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular event responsible for the development of this aggressive lymphoma. The combination of CLL history, transformation to a large cell lymphoma, cytogenetic abnormality (t(14;18)), and evidence of SHM and class switch recombination points towards a dysregulation of the molecular machinery involved in these processes.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **B Cell Development & Activation:** B cells undergo V(D)J recombination in the bone marrow to generate diverse B cell receptors (BCRs). Upon antigen encounter and T cell help in germinal centers, B cells undergo SHM (introducing point mutations in the V region genes) and Class Switch Recombination (CSR) (changing the constant region isotype, e.g., from IgM to IgG, IgA, or IgE) to improve antibody affinity and effector function.
- **Role of AID:** Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase (AID) is an enzyme crucial for both SHM and CSR. It initiates these processes by deaminating cytosine to uracil in single-stranded DNA. Uracil is then removed by uracil DNA glycosylase (UNG), creating abasic sites. These abasic sites are processed by AP endonuclease 1 (APE1), leading to strand breaks.
    - **SHM:** Strand breaks in the V region genes are repaired by the error-prone non-homologous end joining (NHEJ) pathway, introducing point mutations.
    - **CSR:** Strand breaks upstream of switch (S) regions are repaired by the error-prone NHEJ pathway, leading to deletion of the intervening DNA and joining of the VDJ segment to a new constant region gene.
- **Dysregulation of AID:** Aberrant or dysregulated AID activity can lead to genomic instability and chromosomal translocations, particularly involving the IGH locus. This is a known mechanism contributing to the development of B cell lymphomas, including follicular lymphoma (often associated with t(14;18)) and Burkitt lymphoma. The high levels of SHM and frequent switch regions observed in the biopsy support the involvement of AID dysregulation.
- **CLL Transformation:** Richter's transformation is the development of an aggressive lymphoma (most commonly DLBCL) in a patient with CLL. It is often associated with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) infection and can involve genetic alterations similar to those seen in de novo lymphomas, including dysregulation of AID.

##### 3. Linking Clues to the Correct Answer:
The patient's history of CLL, transformation to an aggressive lymphoma with features suggestive of DLBCL (large atypical cells), the presence of t(14;18), and molecular evidence of high SHM and CSR activity strongly implicate a defect in the B cell activation machinery, specifically the enzyme responsible for initiating these processes: AID. Dysregulated AID activity can lead to errors in DNA repair during SHM and CSR, resulting in genomic instability, translocations (like t(14;18)), and ultimately, lymphoma development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Failure of RAG1/RAG2 complex:** RAG1/RAG2 initiates V(D)J recombination in the bone marrow. Failure of this process would lead to a block in B cell development, resulting in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a lack of mature B cells, not a transformed lymphoma with high SHM and CSR activity. This option describes a developmental defect, not a process occurring during activation.
- (B) **Aberrant expression of TdT:** TdT (terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase) is involved in V(D)J recombination by adding P-nucleotides to the ends of DNA segments. While errors in TdT activity can lead to aberrant recombination, the specific finding of high SHM and frequent switch regions points more directly to problems with AID-mediated processes occurring *after* successful V(D)J recombination and during germinal center reactions. TdT is primarily active during V(D)J recombination in the bone marrow, not CSR. While TdT can be aberrantly expressed in some lymphomas, it doesn't directly explain the high SHM and CSR activity seen here.
- (D) **Defective function of the DNA mismatch repair (MMR) system:** MMR defects lead to microsatellite instability and increased mutation rates, particularly in repetitive sequences. While MMR defects can contribute to cancer development, they are not the primary mechanism driving SHM and CSR, which are specifically initiated by AID and involve distinct DNA repair pathways (NHEJ). MMR defects are more commonly associated with Lynch syndrome and certain types of colorectal cancer.
- (E) **Inactivation of the PAX5 transcription factor:** PAX5 is a master regulator of B cell development, essential for the transition from pro-B to pre-B cells and for maintaining B cell identity. Inactivation of PAX5 leads to a block in B cell development, often resulting in a pre-B cell leukemia or a lack of mature B cells. It does not explain the features of a transformed lymphoma with high SHM and CSR activity, which occur in mature B cells undergoing activation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - AID Dysregulation | Option (A) - RAG Failure | Option (B) - TdT Aberrancy | Option (D) - MMR Defect | Option (E) - PAX5 Inactivation |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Process Affected**     | SHM, CSR                               | V(D)J Recombination     | V(D)J Recombination       | General Mutation Rate  | B Cell Development            |
| **Cell Stage Affected** | Mature B cells (Germinal Center)       | Immature B cells (BM)   | Immature B cells (BM)     | All dividing cells     | Immature B cells (BM)          |
| **Key Molecular Event** | Cytidine Deamination                   | P-nucleotide addition   | P-nucleotide addition     | Mismatch Repair        | Transcription Factor Regulation |
| **Consequence**         | Genomic Instability, Translocations, SHM, CSR | SCID, No B cells       | Aberrant Recombination    | Microsatellite Instability | Blocked B cell maturation      |
| **Clinical Relevance**  | B cell lymphomas (e.g., Follicular, Burkitt, DLBCL transformation) | SCID                    | Some lymphomas          | Lynch Syndrome, Colorectal Cancer | Pre-B cell leukemia            |
| **Vignette Fit**        | High SHM, CSR, t(14;18), transformed lymphoma | No                    | Less likely              | No                    | No                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
AID is essential for both somatic hypermutation and class switch recombination in activated B cells. Dysregulation of AID activity leads to genomic instability and is a key driver of B cell lymphoma development, particularly those involving immunoglobulin locus translocations like t(14;18). Remember the distinct roles of RAG1/RAG2 (V(D)J recombination), TdT (P-nucleotide addition during V(D)J), MMR (microsatellite stability), and PAX5 (B cell lineage commitment) in lymphocyte development and function.

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 34 (rigid.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of V(D)J recombination, somatic hypermutation, and class switch recombination in B cell development and activation, specifically the role of activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) and its consequences, including immunoglobulin gene diversification and potential for oncogenesis. (理解B细胞发育和激活过程中V(D)J重组、体细胞高变异和类别转换重组的过程，特别是激活诱导胞嘧啶脱氨酶(AID)的作用及其后果，包括免疫球蛋白基因的多样化和潜在的致癌性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 65-year-old male with a history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) presents with rapidly progressive B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma (NHL). He was previously treated with fludarabine and cyclophosphamide, followed by a stem cell transplant from an HLA-matched unrelated donor 5 years ago. His post-transplant course was initially uneventful, but he developed recurrent sinopulmonary infections over the past year, attributed to hypogammaglobulinemia. Flow cytometry of his peripheral blood reveals a monoclonal B-cell population expressing CD5, CD19, CD20 (dim), and CD23, consistent with CLL. Bone marrow biopsy shows infiltration by large, atypical B cells with high proliferative activity (Ki-67 index > 80%). Cytogenetic analysis of the lymphoma cells reveals a translocation t(14;18)(q32;q21). Further molecular studies show high levels of somatic hypermutation within the immunoglobulin variable region genes of the lymphoma cells, despite the absence of recent antigenic stimulation. The patient's serum immunoglobulin levels are markedly reduced, particularly IgG and IgA.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for the high rate of somatic hypermutation observed in this patient's lymphoma cells?

(A) Aberrant expression of terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT) due to defects in V(D)J recombination repair pathways.
(B) Dysregulation of the mismatch repair (MMR) system leading to increased mutation rates during DNA replication.
(C) Overexpression of activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) in the absence of normal B cell receptor signaling and T cell help.
(D) Increased activity of uracil-DNA glycosylase (UNG) leading to enhanced error-prone base excision repair.
(E) Defective expression of BCL2 leading to increased apoptosis and subsequent genomic instability during cell division.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 65-year-old male with CLL history, post-stem cell transplant, recurrent infections, hypogammaglobulinemia. This suggests underlying immune dysfunction, potentially related to CLL or post-transplant complications.
- **Lymphoma Presentation:** Rapidly progressive B-cell NHL, large atypical cells, high Ki-67 (proliferation), t(14;18) translocation. The t(14;18) translocation juxtaposes the BCL2 gene (on chromosome 18) with the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus (on chromosome 14), leading to overexpression of BCL2, an anti-apoptotic protein. This is a common finding in follicular lymphoma, but can occur in other B-cell lymphomas, especially post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorders (PTLD).
- **Immunophenotype:** CLL phenotype (CD5+, CD19+, CD20dim, CD23+) indicates the lymphoma likely arose from the underlying CLL clone or is a related B-cell malignancy.
- **Key Finding:** High levels of somatic hypermutation (SHM) in Ig variable regions *despite* absence of recent antigenic stimulation. SHM normally occurs in germinal centers during affinity maturation, driven by AID. Its presence in this context, especially in a post-transplant setting with potential immune dysregulation, points to aberrant AID activity.
- **Hypogammaglobulinemia:** Consistent with B cell dysfunction, potentially related to CLL or post-transplant immune reconstitution failure.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Somatic Hypermutation (SHM):** A process occurring in activated B cells within germinal centers. It introduces point mutations into the variable (V) regions of immunoglobulin genes (VH and VL), leading to increased antibody affinity for the antigen.
- **Mechanism of SHM:** Initiated by Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase (AID). AID is an enzyme expressed specifically in activated B cells. It deaminates cytosine (C) to uracil (U) in single-stranded DNA within the V regions of Ig genes.
- **DNA Repair Pathway:** The resulting U:G mismatches are recognized by the DNA repair machinery. Primarily, uracil-DNA glycosylase (UNG) removes the uracil, creating an abasic (AP) site. AP endonuclease 1 (APE1) cleaves the DNA backbone at the AP site. Error-prone DNA polymerases (like Pol η or Pol ζ) and DNA ligase IIIα/XRCC1 complex fill the gap, often introducing mutations (transitions or transversions) during the repair process. Alternatively, the U:G mismatch can be repaired by the mismatch repair (MMR) system (MSH2/MSH6, MLH1/PMS2), which can also lead to mutations, especially if the repair is error-prone.
- **Role of AID:** AID is crucial for both SHM and class switch recombination (CSR). Its aberrant expression or dysregulation can lead to increased mutation rates and potentially oncogenesis (e.g., B-cell lymphomas).
- **Post-Transplant Lymphoproliferative Disorder (PTLD):** A known complication after stem cell transplantation, often associated with Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) infection and immunosuppression. PTLD frequently involves B cells and can exhibit high rates of SHM, sometimes driven by dysregulated AID activity in the context of altered immune regulation.
- **CLL Transformation:** CLL can transform into more aggressive lymphomas, often diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL). These transformed lymphomas frequently show high SHM rates.
- **The Question:** The question asks for the mechanism behind the *high rate* of SHM in the *absence of recent antigenic stimulation*. This points directly to dysregulated AID activity, which is the primary initiator of SHM.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT) is involved in V(D)J recombination, primarily in developing T and B cells in the bone marrow. It adds random nucleotides (N-nucleotides) to the ends of V, D, and J segments. While defects in V(D)J recombination repair can occur, TdT itself is not directly responsible for SHM, which occurs later in activated B cells. Aberrant TdT expression is not the primary driver of SHM in mature B cells.
- (B): The mismatch repair (MMR) system is involved in repairing DNA mismatches, including those created by AID during SHM. However, MMR itself is not the *initiator* of SHM. While defects in MMR can increase mutation rates generally, the specific high rate of SHM in Ig V regions is initiated by AID. Furthermore, the question specifies SHM in Ig genes, which is specifically linked to AID.
- (D): Uracil-DNA glycosylase (UNG) is part of the base excision repair pathway that removes uracil from DNA. It acts *after* AID deaminates cytosine to uracil. While UNG is essential for the SHM process (along with APE1 and error-prone polymerases), it is not the *initiator* of the process. Increased UNG activity alone wouldn't explain the high SHM rate; AID must first create the U:G mismatches.
- (E): BCL2 is an anti-apoptotic protein. Its overexpression, often due to the t(14;18) translocation, promotes cell survival and is characteristic of follicular lymphoma and some other B-cell lymphomas, including PTLD. While BCL2 overexpression contributes to lymphoma development and progression, it does not directly cause somatic hypermutation. Increased cell division due to BCL2 overexpression might increase the *opportunity* for mutations, but it doesn't explain the specific mechanism of SHM initiated by AID.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Somatic Hypermutation (SHM)                                  | V(D)J Recombination                                   | Mismatch Repair (MMR)                               | Base Excision Repair (BER)                             | BCL2 Overexpression                                  |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| **Location**            | Germinal Centers, Activated B cells                          | Bone Marrow, Developing B & T cells                    | Throughout the genome                                 | Throughout the genome                                 | Various cell types, especially lymphocytes            |
| **Timing**              | After B cell activation                                      | During lymphocyte development                          | Continuous                                            | Continuous                                            | Throughout cell life                                  |
| **Initiator**           | AID (deaminates C to U)                                      | RAG1/RAG2 enzymes                                     | Recognition of mismatches (e.g., U:G)                  | Recognition of damaged bases (e.g., Uracil)           | N/A (promotes survival)                               |
| **Key Enzymes/Proteins** | AID, UNG, APE1, Error-prone DNA polymerases (Pol η, Pol ζ) | RAG1/RAG2, TdT, Artemis, DNA Ligase IV, NHEJ factors | MSH2/MSH6, MLH1/PMS2                                  | UNG, APE1, DNA Polymerase β, DNA Ligase IIIα/XRCC1    | N/A                                                   |
| **Target**              | Ig V region genes                                             | Ig V, D, J genes; TCR V, D, J genes                    | DNA mismatches                                        | Damaged/modified bases                                | N/A                                                   |
| **Outcome**             | Increased Ig affinity, potential oncogenesis                 | Generation of diverse Ig/TCR repertoires              | Correction of mismatches (can be error-prone)         | Repair of damaged bases (can be error-prone)         | Increased cell survival, lymphoma development         |
| **Relevance to Case**   | High SHM rate in lymphoma despite no stimulation             | N/A (occurs earlier)                                  | Involved in processing AID-induced lesions            | Involved in processing AID-induced lesions            | Contributes to lymphoma survival (t(14;18))             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The high rate of somatic hypermutation (SHM) in immunoglobulin genes is initiated by the enzyme Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase (AID) in activated B cells. Dysregulation of AID can lead to increased mutation rates and contribute to B-cell lymphomagenesis, particularly in post-transplant settings or in transformed lymphomas. Key differentials for SHM mechanisms include V(D)J recombination (TdT), DNA repair pathways (MMR, BER involving UNG), and factors promoting cell survival (BCL2). Understanding the specific role of AID in SHM is crucial for understanding B cell development, antibody affinity maturation, and the pathogenesis of certain B-cell malignancies.

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 35 (membrane)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of MHC class I molecules in T cell selection (positive and negative) and their expression on various cell types, including thymocytes and peripheral cells, is crucial for distinguishing between normal immune function and immunodeficiency states or autoimmune phenomena. (理解 MHC I 类分子在 T 细胞选择（阳性选择和阴性选择）中的作用，以及它们在各种细胞类型上的表达，包括胸腺细胞和外周细胞，对于区分正常的免疫功能和免疫缺陷状态或自身免疫现象至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy with a history of recurrent severe bacterial infections (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*) since infancy is evaluated. He has never received vaccinations. Physical examination reveals failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea. Laboratory studies show normal B cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels, but markedly reduced numbers of CD8+ T cells (approximately 5% of total lymphocytes, normal >50%) and normal numbers of CD4+ T cells. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals absent expression of MHC class I molecules on all nucleated cells, including monocytes, fibroblasts, and lymphocytes. His mother and father are healthy and have normal MHC class I expression. A skin biopsy from the patient shows a lack of cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) infiltration in response to a viral skin infection.

(A) Defective expression of the CD3 complex on T cells.
(B) Mutation in the gene encoding the T cell receptor (TCR) beta chain.
(C) Defective TAP1 or TAP2 transporter protein.
(D) Mutation in the gene encoding the beta-2 microglobulin (β2m) protein.
(E) Defective expression of MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs).

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections:** Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity, particularly T cell function, as T cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens and coordinating immune responses against bacteria.
- **Failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea:** Common manifestations of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T cell function.
- **Normal B cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels:** Rules out defects primarily affecting B cell development or function, such as X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) or common variable immunodeficiency (CVID). This points towards a T cell-specific defect or a defect affecting T cell help for B cells (which is not the case here as Ig levels are normal).
- **Markedly reduced CD8+ T cells, normal CD4+ T cells:** This specific pattern is highly suggestive of a defect in MHC class I presentation, as CD8+ T cells (CTLs) recognize antigens presented by MHC class I, while CD4+ T cells recognize antigens presented by MHC class II.
- **Absent MHC class I expression on all nucleated cells:** This is the critical finding. MHC class I molecules are essential for presenting endogenous antigens (like viral proteins or tumor antigens) to CD8+ T cells. Their absence prevents the development and survival of CD8+ T cells.
- **Normal MHC class I expression in parents:** Indicates a genetic defect in the patient, likely autosomal recessive, affecting the patient's ability to express MHC class I.
- **Lack of CTL infiltration in skin biopsy:** Confirms the functional consequence of absent MHC class I and reduced CD8+ T cells – impaired cytotoxic T cell response.
- **The question asks for the underlying defect:** Given the absent MHC class I expression on all nucleated cells and the specific reduction in CD8+ T cells, we need to identify the component required for MHC class I surface expression.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **MHC Class I Structure and Function:** MHC class I molecules consist of a heavy chain (alpha chain, encoded by HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C genes) and a smaller protein called beta-2 microglobulin (β2m, encoded by a gene on chromosome 15). They present peptides derived from intracellular proteins to CD8+ T cells.
- **T Cell Development and Selection:**
    - **Positive Selection (Thymus Cortex):** Thymocytes expressing TCRs that can weakly bind self-MHC molecules (either class I or class II) receive survival signals. This ensures that mature T cells can recognize MHC molecules on peripheral cells. MHC class I presentation is crucial for selecting CD8+ T cells, while MHC class II presentation selects CD4+ T cells.
    - **Negative Selection (Thymus Medulla):** Thymocytes that bind self-MHC molecules with high affinity are eliminated (apoptosis) to prevent autoimmunity.
- **Role of β2m:** Beta-2 microglobulin is essential for the proper folding, assembly, and transport of the MHC class I heavy chain to the cell surface. Without β2m, the MHC class I heavy chain is retained in the endoplasmic reticulum and degraded.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Absence of MHC class I leads to a failure of positive selection for CD8+ T cells in the thymus, resulting in their severe deficiency in the periphery. CD4+ T cells, which rely on MHC class II for selection, are relatively spared. The lack of MHC class I on all nucleated cells also impairs the presentation of endogenous antigens, leading to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens (viruses, some bacteria) and potentially tumors. The patient's presentation fits this pattern.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** Defects in MHC class I expression can be caused by mutations in the HLA genes (rarely causing complete absence on all cells) or, more commonly, by mutations in the β2m gene or genes encoding components of the antigen processing machinery (like TAP1/TAP2).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective expression of the CD3 complex on T cells:** The CD3 complex is part of the TCR complex and is essential for signal transduction upon TCR engagement. Defects in CD3 (e.g., CD3δ chain deficiency) cause severe T cell immunodeficiency, affecting both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but MHC class I expression would be normal. This doesn't fit the specific CD8+ deficiency and normal MHC class I expression on APCs.
- (B) **Mutation in the gene encoding the TCR beta chain:** Mutations in the TCR beta chain gene can lead to defects in TCR assembly and T cell development, potentially causing SCID or T cell deficiency. However, MHC class I expression would be normal, and the specific pattern of CD8+ deficiency wouldn't be explained.
- (C) **Defective TAP1 or TAP2 transporter protein:** TAP1 and TAP2 are essential for transporting peptides from the cytosol into the endoplasmic reticulum, where they bind to MHC class I molecules. Defects in TAP1/TAP2 (e.g., Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type I) lead to impaired presentation of *most* endogenous peptides by MHC class I, resulting in reduced surface expression of MHC class I molecules. This causes a similar phenotype: reduced CD8+ T cells and increased susceptibility to certain viral infections. However, TAP defects typically lead to *reduced* MHC class I expression, not complete absence on *all* nucleated cells. Furthermore, TAP defects primarily affect peptide loading, while β2m defects affect the structural integrity and transport of the entire MHC class I complex. The vignette describes *absent* MHC class I expression on *all* nucleated cells, which is more characteristic of β2m deficiency.
- (E) **Defective expression of MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs):** Defects in MHC class II expression (e.g., Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome Type II, caused by mutations in CIITA or HLA-DM genes) primarily affect CD4+ T cell development and function, leading to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). MHC class I expression and CD8+ T cell numbers would be normal. This is the opposite of the patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | MHC Class I Deficiency (e.g., β2m mutation) | MHC Class II Deficiency (e.g., CIITA mutation) | TAP Deficiency (e.g., TAP1/TAP2 mutation) | CD3 Deficiency | TCR β Chain Mutation |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- | :------------- | :-------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | MHC Class I assembly/transport             | MHC Class II assembly/transport              | Peptide transport into ER                | TCR signaling  | TCR assembly         |
| **MHC Class I Expression**  | Absent/Reduced on all nucleated cells      | Normal                                      | Reduced                                  | Normal         | Normal                |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Normal                                      | Absent/Reduced on APCs                      | Normal                                  | Normal         | Normal                |
| **CD4+ T Cells**            | Normal                                      | Severely reduced/absent                     | Normal                                  | Reduced/Absent | Reduced/Absent        |
| **CD8+ T Cells**            | Severely reduced/absent                     | Normal                                      | Reduced                                  | Reduced/Absent | Reduced/Absent        |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Recurrent intracellular infections          | SCID (recurrent bacterial, viral, fungal)   | Susceptibility to certain viruses        | SCID           | T cell deficiency     |
| **Key Gene**                | *B2M*                                       | *CIITA*, *HLA-DM*                            | *TAP1*, *TAP2*                           | *CD3D*, etc.   | *TRBC1*, etc.         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The absence of MHC class I molecules on all nucleated cells leads to a failure of positive selection for CD8+ T cells in the thymus, resulting in their severe deficiency. Beta-2 microglobulin (β2m) is an essential component of MHC class I molecules, and its absence prevents proper MHC class I surface expression. Differentiate this from defects in MHC class II (affecting CD4+ T cells), TAP transporters (affecting peptide loading, usually reducing MHC I expression), CD3 complex (affecting T cell signaling), or TCR components (affecting T cell development more broadly).

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 36 (receptors)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of T cell receptors (TCRs) in T cell development, specifically the process of positive and negative selection in the thymus, and the consequences of defects in this process, including autoimmunity and immunodeficiency. (理解T细胞受体(TCR)在T细胞发育中的作用，特别是胸腺中的阳性选择和阴性选择过程，以及该过程中缺陷的后果，包括自身免疫和免疫缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and chronic diarrhea, starting shortly after birth. He has a history of failure to thrive and eczema. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died at age 10 from complications of a severe, disseminated viral infection. Physical examination reveals lymphopenia and hepatosplenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a markedly reduced number of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but normal numbers of B cells and NK cells. Further immunological testing reveals that the patient's T cells are unable to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation (e.g., PHA) or specific antigens, even in the presence of exogenous IL-2. Thymic shadow is absent on chest X-ray. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *CD3D* gene, which encodes the delta chain of the T cell co-receptor complex.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient's T cell development?

(A) Failure of positive selection due to inability to recognize MHC class I molecules.
(B) Failure of negative selection leading to autoreactive T cells.
(C) Defective rearrangement of the TCR beta chain gene.
(D) Impaired expression of CD4 and CD8 co-receptors on thymocytes.
(E) Defective signaling downstream of the TCR due to a mutation in the zeta chain.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe infections, failure to thrive, eczema, lymphopenia, hepatosplenomegaly:** Suggests a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a severe T cell defect.
- **Onset shortly after birth:** Points towards a congenital immunodeficiency.
- **Family history of severe disseminated viral infection:** Suggests a possible genetic basis for immunodeficiency, potentially X-linked or autosomal recessive.
- **Reduced CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, normal B and NK cells:** Indicates a specific defect in T cell development or survival, characteristic of T-B+NK+ SCID.
- **T cells unable to proliferate in response to mitogens or antigens, even with IL-2:** Suggests a defect in the T cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway or the TCR itself, as IL-2 signaling is bypassed.
- **Absent thymic shadow:** Indicates thymic hypoplasia or aplasia, common in SCID.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *CD3D* gene:** This gene encodes the CD3 delta chain, a component of the T cell co-receptor complex (CD3). The CD3 complex is essential for transducing signals from the TCR to the T cell.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Development:** T cell precursors migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus. In the thymus, they undergo maturation, involving TCR gene rearrangement and selection processes.
- **TCR Gene Rearrangement:** T cells express TCRs composed of alpha (α) and beta (β) chains, or gamma (γ) and delta (δ) chains. Successful rearrangement of these genes is required for TCR expression.
- **Positive Selection:** Thymocytes expressing functional TCRs that can weakly bind self-MHC molecules (MHC class I or class II) on thymic epithelial cells receive survival signals (mediated by CD3 complex signaling). Thymocytes that cannot bind MHC undergo apoptosis (death by neglect). This ensures that mature T cells are MHC-restricted.
- **Negative Selection:** Thymocytes that bind self-MHC molecules presenting self-peptides with high affinity are eliminated via apoptosis (clonal deletion). This process prevents autoimmunity.
- **CD3 Complex:** The CD3 complex (composed of γ, δ, ε, and ζ chains) is non-covalently associated with the TCR αβ or γδ heterodimer on the cell surface. It is crucial for signal transduction. When the TCR binds to its cognate peptide-MHC complex, the CD3 complex initiates intracellular signaling cascades, leading to T cell activation.
- **CD4 and CD8 Co-receptors:** These molecules are expressed on thymocytes and mature T cells. CD4 binds MHC class II, and CD8 binds MHC class I. They enhance TCR signaling and are essential for T cell activation and function.
- **Patient's Defect:** The mutation in *CD3D* leads to a deficiency in the CD3 delta chain. This disrupts the formation or function of the entire CD3 complex. Without a functional CD3 complex, TCR signaling is severely impaired, even if the TCR itself is capable of binding MHC.
- **Consequences:**
    - **Impaired Positive Selection:** Thymocytes with rearranged TCRs cannot receive the necessary survival signals via the CD3 complex when interacting with self-MHC, leading to apoptosis during positive selection. This results in a profound deficiency of both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells.
    - **Impaired Negative Selection:** While negative selection might be less effective, the primary defect is the failure of positive selection and the inability of surviving thymocytes to mature and function properly due to defective signaling.
    - **Clinical Presentation:** The lack of functional T cells leads to severe immunodeficiency, characterized by susceptibility to opportunistic infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, protozoal), failure to thrive, and chronic diarrhea. The absence of T cell help also impairs B cell function, although B cell numbers may be normal initially.

##### 3. Linking the Defect to the Options
- The mutation in *CD3D* directly affects the CD3 complex, which is essential for TCR signaling.
- This defective signaling impairs the ability of thymocytes to respond to MHC binding during positive selection.
- Therefore, the primary defect is the failure of positive selection due to the inability to transduce signals through the TCR complex.
- The lack of functional CD3 complex also prevents proper expression or stabilization of CD4 and CD8 co-receptors on the cell surface of developing thymocytes, although the question asks for the *underlying* defect. The inability to signal through the TCR complex prevents the downstream events necessary for full maturation and co-receptor expression.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Failure of positive selection due to inability to recognize MHC class I molecules.** This is incorrect. The patient's defect is not in the TCR's ability to recognize MHC (the TCR itself is likely functional, just not signaled properly), but in the *signaling* downstream of the TCR. The CD3 complex is required for signaling *after* MHC recognition. While positive selection is impaired, the reason is defective signaling, not inability to recognize MHC.
- (B) **Failure of negative selection leading to autoreactive T cells.** This is incorrect. The primary defect is in positive selection, leading to a lack of mature T cells, not an excess of autoreactive ones. While negative selection might also be affected, the overwhelming phenotype is severe T cell deficiency. Furthermore, mutations in genes involved in negative selection (like *AIRE*) typically present with autoimmune polyendocrinopathy-candidiasis-ectodermal dystrophy (APECED), not SCID.
- (C) **Defective rearrangement of the TCR beta chain gene.** This is incorrect. While defective TCR rearrangement can cause SCID (e.g., mutations in *RAG1/2* genes), the vignette specifically states a mutation in the *CD3D* gene, which is part of the co-receptor complex, not the TCR chain itself. Defects in TCR rearrangement typically lead to a lack of TCR expression, while in this case, the TCR is likely expressed but non-functional due to lack of signaling.
- (E) **Defective signaling downstream of the TCR due to a mutation in the zeta chain.** This is incorrect. The zeta chain (CD3ζ) is a crucial component of the CD3 complex. A mutation in the zeta chain would indeed cause defective signaling downstream of the TCR, leading to a similar phenotype (SCID). However, the vignette specifically states a mutation in the *CD3D* gene (delta chain), not the zeta chain. While both are components of CD3 and lead to similar outcomes, the specific gene mutated points to option (D) as the most accurate description of the *underlying* defect in this specific case. Option (D) describes the consequence of the CD3D mutation on the co-receptors, which is a direct result of the defective signaling complex.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Patient's Condition (CD3D mutation) | Failure of Positive Selection (General) | Failure of Negative Selection (General) | Defective TCR Rearrangement (e.g., RAG deficiency) | Defective Zeta Chain (CD3ζ mutation) |
|-------------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| **Gene Defect**         | *CD3D*                               | Various (e.g., MHC genes, TCR genes)    | Various (e.g., *AIRE*)                  | *RAG1*, *RAG2*                                  | *CD3ζ*                                |
| **Primary Defect**      | TCR signaling                         | Inability to recognize MHC              | Inability to eliminate autoreactive cells | Lack of TCR expression                          | TCR signaling                          |
| **Selection Process**   | Impaired Positive Selection           | Impaired Positive Selection             | Impaired Negative Selection             | Impaired Positive Selection                     | Impaired Positive Selection           |
| **T Cell Numbers**      | Severely Reduced (CD4-, CD8-)        | Severely Reduced (CD4-, CD8-)            | Normal/Increased Autoreactive T cells    | Severely Reduced (CD4-, CD8-)                    | Severely Reduced (CD4-, CD8-)          |
| **Autoimmunity**        | Usually Absent (due to lack of T cells) | Usually Absent                          | Often Present (APECED)                  | Usually Absent                                  | Usually Absent                          |
| **Clinical Phenotype**  | SCID                                 | SCID                                    | Autoimmune Disease                      | SCID                                            | SCID                                  |
| **CD4/CD8 Expression** | Impaired                             | N/A                                     | N/A                                     | Impaired (if TCR not expressed)                | Impaired                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The CD3 complex is essential for transducing signals from the TCR upon MHC recognition during T cell development. Defects in CD3 components (like CD3δ or CD3ζ) impair positive selection in the thymus, leading to severe T cell deficiency (SCID). This contrasts with defects in TCR gene rearrangement (lack of TCR expression) or defects in negative selection (autoimmunity). Remember that positive selection ensures T cells can recognize MHC, while negative selection eliminates self-reactive T cells.

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Lymphocyte Development and Selection: Part 3 Focus 37 (lymphocytes)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the process of V(D)J recombination, somatic hypermutation, and class switch recombination in B cell development and activation, and how defects or alterations in these processes lead to specific immunodeficiencies or malignancies. (理解B细胞发育和激活过程中V(D)J重组、体细胞超突变和类别转换重组的过程，以及这些过程中的缺陷或改变如何导致特定的免疫缺陷或恶性肿瘤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting shortly after 6 months of age. He has a history of multiple episodes of *Haemophilus influenzae* type b (Hib) meningitis despite receiving the standard childhood vaccination schedule, including the Hib conjugate vaccine. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and splenomegaly. Laboratory studies show markedly reduced serum levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE, but normal levels of IgM. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a significantly decreased number of B cells (CD19+ cells). Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *AICDA* gene.

Which of the following cellular processes is most likely defective in this patient?

(A) Somatic hypermutation
(B) Class switch recombination
(C) V(D)J recombination
(D) Affinity maturation
(E) T-cell receptor gene rearrangement

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections:** Suggests a humoral immunodeficiency, as antibodies are crucial for defense against encapsulated bacteria like *H. influenzae*.
- **Onset after 6 months:** Maternal IgG wanes around this time, unmasking underlying B cell defects.
- **Hib meningitis despite vaccination:** Hib conjugate vaccines induce a T-cell dependent antibody response (IgG, IgA, IgE). Failure to mount an effective response despite vaccination points to a defect in B cell function or T cell help, specifically affecting isotype switching.
- **Reduced IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM:** This pattern is characteristic of Hyper-IgM syndrome. IgM is the first antibody produced, but switching to other isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgE) is impaired.
- **Decreased B cells (CD19+):** Suggests a problem with B cell development or survival, although some forms of Hyper-IgM syndrome have normal B cell numbers. However, the combination with isotype switching defect is key.
- **Mutation in *AICDA* gene:** AICDA (Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase) is essential for both somatic hypermutation and class switch recombination. Its deficiency leads to Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **B Cell Development & Activation:** B cells mature in the bone marrow, undergoing V(D)J recombination to generate diverse B cell receptors (BCRs). Upon antigen encounter and T cell help (CD40L-CD40 interaction, cytokines), B cells undergo clonal expansion, somatic hypermutation (SHM), and class switch recombination (CSR).
- **V(D)J Recombination:** Occurs during B and T cell development in the bone marrow and thymus, respectively. It generates receptor diversity by randomly joining variable (V), diversity (D), and joining (J) gene segments. Errors in V(D)J recombination can lead to immunodeficiencies (e.g., RAG1/2 deficiency) or B cell malignancies.
- **Somatic Hypermutation (SHM):** Occurs in germinal centers after B cell activation. It introduces point mutations into the variable regions of immunoglobulin genes, increasing antibody affinity. AICDA is crucial for SHM, initiating the process by deaminating cytosine to uracil.
- **Class Switch Recombination (CSR):** Also occurs in germinal centers. It changes the constant region of the heavy chain, altering the antibody isotype (e.g., IgM to IgG, IgA, or IgE) while preserving the variable region and antigen specificity. AICDA is also essential for CSR, initiating the process. CSR allows antibodies to perform different effector functions (e.g., IgG for opsonization, IgE for allergy/parasites).
- **Hyper-IgM Syndromes:** A group of primary immunodeficiencies characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. This results from defects in the CSR pathway.
    - **X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L deficiency):** Most common form. Defect in CD40L on T cells prevents interaction with CD40 on B cells, blocking T cell help required for CSR.
    - **AD-Hyper-IgM (AICDA deficiency):** Autosomal recessive. Deficiency in AICDA enzyme prevents both SHM and CSR.
    - **Other forms:** Defects in UNG (uracil-N-glycosylase), DNA repair enzymes, or CD40 itself.
- **AICDA Function:** AICDA initiates both SHM and CSR by converting cytosine to uracil in DNA. Uracil is then removed by UNG, creating an abasic site. This site is processed by DNA repair pathways (base excision repair or mismatch repair) leading to mutations (SHM) or DNA breaks (CSR).
- **Patient's Condition:** The patient has low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM, decreased B cells, and an *AICDA* mutation. This clinical picture is consistent with Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2 due to AICDA deficiency. Since AICDA is required for both SHM and CSR, both processes are defective. However, the most direct consequence explaining the low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE is the failure of **Class Switch Recombination (CSR)**.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Somatic hypermutation: While AICDA deficiency *does* impair SHM, the primary clinical consequence explaining the lack of IgG, IgA, and IgE is the failure of CSR. SHM primarily affects antibody affinity, not isotype production. Patients with impaired SHM but intact CSR can still produce IgG, IgA, IgE, albeit potentially with lower affinity. Therefore, CSR defect is the more direct explanation for the observed immunoglobulin profile.
- (C) V(D)J recombination: This process occurs early in B cell development to generate receptor diversity. Defects in V(D)J recombination (e.g., RAG1/2 deficiency) typically lead to a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) with a profound lack of both B and T cells, presenting much earlier and more severely than this patient's presentation. This patient has decreased B cells but likely has normal T cells (not explicitly tested but implied by the context of T-dependent vaccine failure).
- (D) Affinity maturation: This is the process by which antibody affinity increases over time, driven by SHM and selection of B cells with higher affinity receptors. It is dependent on SHM. While impaired in AICDA deficiency, it's not the primary defect explaining the lack of IgG, IgA, IgE. The core problem is the inability to switch isotypes.
- (E) T-cell receptor gene rearrangement: This is analogous to V(D)J recombination but occurs in T cells in the thymus. Defects in T cell receptor rearrangement lead to T cell deficiencies, not the specific B cell isotype switching defect seen here.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | V(D)J Recombination Defect | AICDA Deficiency (Hyper-IgM 2) | CD40L Deficiency (Hyper-IgM 1) |
|-------------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| **Affected Cells**       | B & T cells               | B cells                        | B cells                        |
| **Primary Defect**      | Receptor diversity        | CSR & SHM                      | T cell help for CSR           |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels**| Absent B/T cells -> Absent all Ig | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE |
| **B Cell Numbers**      | Severely reduced/absent   | Variable (often reduced)        | Normal                          |
| **T Cell Numbers**      | Severely reduced/absent   | Normal                          | Normal                          |
| **Clinical Presentation**| SCID (early, severe)      | Recurrent infections (later)    | Recurrent infections (later)    |
| **Gene Involved**       | RAG1, RAG2                | AICDA                          | CD40L                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The hallmark of Hyper-IgM syndromes is the inability to switch immunoglobulin isotypes, leading to normal or elevated IgM and low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. Defects can occur in the B cell (e.g., AICDA, UNG deficiency) or the T cell (CD40L deficiency) components of the class switch recombination pathway. AICDA deficiency impairs both class switch recombination and somatic hypermutation. Differentiating Hyper-IgM syndromes from other humoral immunodeficiencies (like X-linked agammaglobulinemia, CVID) relies on the characteristic IgM profile and understanding the underlying molecular defect.
